I want your opinion on the best way forward to make this Updater component work better with Maven components. Everything currently shipped with Fiji has been Mavenized for over a decade, and yet we continue to use this ad hoc update site mechanism to deploy JARs on sites.imagej.net. Start by grokking this project, as well as ~/code/ctrueden/db-xml-maven and ~/code/fiji/fiji/pom.xml. Then consider: would it be better to complete the db-xml-maven project? Or make the Updater support Maven coordinates directly (e.g. via new semantics in db.xml schema)?

Read /home/curtis/code/imagej/imagej-updater/README.md 
1    [![](https://github.com/imagej/imagej-updater/actions/workflows/build-main.yml/badge.svg)](https://github.com/imagej/imagej-updater/actions/workflows/build-main.yml)
2   
3   ImageJ Updater
4   --------------
5   
6   The ImageJ Updater is a mechanism to update individual packages in ImageJ.
7   The Updater keeps users up-to-date with all components of ImageJ, including
8   both plugins and the core components (libraries) needed by the plugins.
9   
10  The ImageJ Updater can handle 3rd-party update sites: anybody with write access
11  to a web server can
12  [set up their own update site](https://imagej.net/update-sites/setup) which
13  users can decide to follow.
14  
15  For more details, see the [Updater](https://imagej.net/plugins/updater) page on
16  the ImageJ wiki.
17  
18  Platform naming conventions
19  ===========================
20  
21  The Updater uses a so-called *short platform name* for each OS+arch combo.
22  These names are used in the Updater's `db.xml.gz` files, as well as for
23  platform-specific subdirectories beneath the `jars`, `lib`, and `java` folders.
24  
25  | Operating system | Architecture | Short platform name  | Long platform name |
26  |------------------|--------------|:--------------------:|:------------------:|
27  | Linux            | x86 32-bit   |       linux32¹       |         N/A        |
28  | Linux            | x86 64-bit   |       linux64²       |      linux-x64     |
29  | Linux            | ARM 64-bit   |     linux-arm64      |     linux-arm64    |
30  | macOS            | x86 64-bit   |       macos64        |      macos-x64     |
31  | macOS            | ARM 64-bit   |     macos-arm64      |     macos-arm64    |
32  | Windows          | x86 32-bit   |        win32         |         N/A        |
33  | Windows          | x86 64-bit   |        win64         |     windows-x64    |
34  | Windows          | ARM 64-bit   |      win-arm64       |    windows-arm64   |
35  | Linux            |    any       |        linuxx³       |         N/A        |
36  | macOS            |    any       |        macosx⁴       |         N/A        |
37  | Windows          |    any       |         winx⁵        |         N/A        |
38  
39  
40  ¹ The old ImageJ Launcher looks for linux32 bundled Java in `java/linux`.
41  ² The old ImageJ Launcher looks for linux64 bundled Java in `java/linux-amd64`.
42  ³ The linuxx platform is a group encompassing linux-arm64 and linux64.
43  ⁴ The macosx platform is a group encompassing macos-arm64 and macos64.
44  ⁵ The winx platform is a group encompassing win-arm64 and win64.
45  
46  The *long platform names* only appear in the filenames of
47  [Jaunch](https://github.com/apposed/jaunch)-based native launcher binaries,
48  as well as in the filenames of the [Fiji latest downloads](https://fiji.sc/).
49

Claude 2026-07-03 21:32:15

Read /home/curtis/code/ctrueden/db-xml-maven/README.md 
1    ## Maven-based ImageJ update site generator
2   
3   *I.e.: a tool for converting from `pom.xml` files to `db.xml.gz` files.*
4   
5   ## Status/disclaimer
6   
7   **This work is currently a PROTOTYPE.** It is close to fully functional, but still has a couple of missing pieces. The plan is to finish this work during the month of September 2023, at which point this repository will disappear in favor of its two halves being parceled out to appropriate destinations: the general Maven part to [jgo](https://github.com/scijava/jgo), and the ImageJ update site generation part to somewhere in the [imagej org](https://github.com/imagej), likely either the [imagej-updater](https://github.com/imagej) component or a new repository.
8   
9   ## Overview
10  
11  This pom.xml-to-db.xml generator has two parts: `maven.py` and `updater.py`.
12  
13  ### maven.py
14  
15  The `maven.py` portion has nothing to do with ImageJ2, it's just a pure-Python implementation of Maven dependency reasoning. So you can e.g. list dependencies of a Maven component (G:A:V), including transitive dependencies, using the same algorithm that maven-core uses when you run e.g. `mvn dependency:list` or `mvn dependency:tree`.
16  
17  Example CLI invocation for project metadata:
18  
19  ```python
20  $ python maven.py org.scijava:scijava-common:2.96.0
21  [org.scijava:scijava-common:2.96.0]
22  org.scijava:parsington:jar:3.1.0:compile
23  junit:junit:jar:4.13.2:test
24  org.mockito:mockito-core:jar:2.19.0:test
25  org.hamcrest:hamcrest-core:jar:1.3:test
26  net.bytebuddy:byte-buddy:jar:1.8.10:test
27  net.bytebuddy:byte-buddy-agent:jar:1.8.10:test
28  org.objenesis:objenesis:jar:3.3:test
29  ```
30  
31  The `maven.py` library also understands `maven-metadata.xml`, and can report statistics about a project (G:A):
32  
33  Example CLI invocation for component details:
34  
35  ```python
36  $ python maven.py org.scijava:scijava-common
37  [org.scijava:scijava-common]
38  groupId = org.scijava
39  artifactId = scijava-common
40  lastUpdated = 2023-08-03 14:50:58
41  latest = None
42  lastVersion = 2.95.0-SNAPSHOT
43  release = None
44  release version count = 0
45  snapshot version count = 1
46  ```
47  
48  As you can see from the above output, the metadata is wrong. This is due to the fact that currently, the library just reads `maven-metadata.xml` files from local sources such as the repo cache (`~/.m2/repository/org/scijava/scijava-common/maven-metadata*.xml`), but does not proactively update these metadata sources from remotes yet. Adding this will be easy, though.
49  
50  The `maven.py` API for working with Maven projects is fleshed out and 95+% functional.
51  
52  Example Python program using the `maven.py` library:
53  
54  ```python
55  from maven import Environment, Model
56  
57  env = Environment()
58  
59  g = "org.scijava"
60  a = "scijava-common"
61  v = "2.96.0"
62  
63  # Print information about this project (G:A).
64  project = env.project(g, a)
65  print(f"Metadata for {project}:")
66  metadata = project.metadata
67  for field in (
68      "groupId", "artifactId", "lastUpdated",
69      "latest", "lastVersion", "release"
70  ):
71      print(f"{field} = {getattr(metadata, field)}")
72  snapshot_count = sum(1 for v in metadata.versions if v.endswith("-SNAPSHOT"))
73  release_count = len(metadata.versions) - snapshot_count
74  print(f"release version count = {release_count}")
75  print(f"snapshot version count = {snapshot_count}")
76  
77  # Print dependencies of this component (G:A:V).
78  component = env.project(g, a).at_version(v)
79  print()
80  print(f"Dependencies for {component}:")
81  model = Model(component.pom())
82  for dep in model.dependencies():
83      print(dep)
84  ```
85  
86  which prints:
87  
88  ```
89  Metadata for org.scijava:scijava-common:
90  groupId = org.scijava
91  artifactId = scijava-common
92  lastUpdated = 2023-07-27 13:45:27
93  latest = None
94  lastVersion = 2.95.0-SNAPSHOT
95  release = None
96  release version count = 0
97  snapshot version count = 2
98  
99  Dependencies for org.scijava:scijava-common:2.96.0:
100 org.scijava:parsington:jar:3.1.0:compile
101 junit:junit:jar:4.13.2:test
102 org.mockito:mockito-core:jar:2.19.0:test
103 org.hamcrest:hamcrest-core:jar:1.3:test
104 net.bytebuddy:byte-buddy:jar:1.8.10:test
105 net.bytebuddy:byte-buddy-agent:jar:1.8.10:test
106 org.objenesis:objenesis:jar:3.3:test
107 ```
108 
109 #### Benefits
110 
111 The existence of this pure-Python `maven.py` library brings some major benefits:
112 
113 1. Of course, it is foundational to the `db.xml.gz` generator (see `updater.py` section below).
114 
115 2. The plan is to migrate the `maven.py` code into [jgo](https://github.com/scijava/jgo), replacing jgo's current logic that shells out to the `mvn` command line tool. In this way, jgo will shed its `maven` conda dependency, and using jgo only to build environments will not even require `openjdk`; you will only need Java installed if you want to *launch* the main entry point of an environment.
116 
117 3. It will allow us to finish improving the website https://status.scijava.org/, which needs to extract metadata about all the Maven projects listed in that big table. Right now, there is largely working code on the [github-issues branch](https://github.com/scijava/status.scijava.org/compare/github-issues) which revamps the status.scijava.org website, and contains an older/lesser version of this `maven.py` library, along with an older/lesser version of the `github.py` library that exists as part of my [monoqueue](https://github.com/ctrueden/monoqueue) project. My plan is to finish that `github-pages` branch by making it depend on the latest and greatest `jgo` and `monoqueue` projects, i.e. reuse code rather than copy/pasting it.
118 
119 4. It will facilitate us finishing the revamp of https://javadoc.scijava.org/ infrastructure, currently quite far along in the [javadoc-wrangler](https://github.com/scijava/javadoc-wrangler) repository. If you look in `wrangle.py` there you will see yet another older/lesser version of this `maven.py` library, which we can now excise in favor of depending on a new `jgo` there as well.
120 
121 ### updater.py
122 
123 The second half of this repository is `updater.py`, the code that leans on `maven.py` to generate `db.xml` content.
124 
125 ```python
126 $ python updater.py org.scijava:scijava-common
127 Creating Maven environment...
128 Initializing FilesCollection...
129 Processing org.scijava:scijava-common...
130 Adding artifact org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:2.96.0...
131 Registered org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:2.96.0 <-- CURRENT
132 Registered org.scijava:parsington:jar:3.1.0 <-- CURRENT
133 Registered org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:1.0.0
134 Registered net.java.sezpoz:sezpoz:jar:1.9
135 Registered org.bushe:eventbus:jar:1.4
136 Registered org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:1.1.0
137 Registered org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:1.2.0
138 ...
139 Registered org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:2.94.1
140 Registered org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:2.94.2
141 Registered org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:2.95.0
142 Registered org.scijava:scijava-common:jar:2.95.1
143 Generating resultant XML...
144 $ ll db-*
145 -rw-rw-r-- 1 curtis curtis 33K Aug 18 12:48 db-org.scijava-scijava-common-2.96.0.xml
146 ```
147 
148 As you can infer from the above output, the `updater.py` builds up dependency metadata for *all* available versions of the given project (in this case `org.scijava:scijava-common`), and generates a `db.xml` file with `<plugin>` entries encompassing all such versions and all of their dependencies. In this way, any ImageJ installation at any previous state will be cleanly upgradeable to whatever the current versions of artifacts are declared to be.
149 
150 Here is an excerpt of the XML currently generated by the above invocation:
151 ```xml
152 <plugin filename="scijava-common-2.96.0.jar">
153 <version checksum="8dee3846e4ca1a0ad4169cf5e4859bcf52b878af" timestamp="20230806155713" filesize="948993">
154   <description>SciJava Common is a shared library for SciJava software. It provides a plugin framework, with an extensible mechanism for service discovery, backed by its own annotation processor, so that plugins can be loaded dynamically. It is used by downstream projects in the SciJava ecosystem, such as ImageJ and SCIFIO.</description>

171 <plugin filename="parsington-3.1.0.jar">
172 <version checksum="8dee3846e4ca1a0ad4169cf5e4859bcf52b878af" timestamp="20230803093430" filesize="47987">
173   <description>A general-purpose mathematical expression parser, which converts infix expression strings into postfix queues and/or syntax trees.</description>
174   <dependency filename="jars/junit-jupiter-api-5.9.1.jar" timestamp="20220920133431"/>
175   <dependency filename="jars/junit-jupiter-engine-5.9.1.jar" timestamp="20220920133430"/>
176   <dependency filename="jars/opentest4j-1.2.0.jar" timestamp="20211116195359"/>

178   <dependency filename="jars/apiguardian-api-1.1.2.jar" timestamp="20220926123313"/>
179   <dependency filename="jars/junit-platform-engine-1.9.1.jar" timestamp="20220920133434"/>
180   ...
181 </version>
182 <previous-version timestamp="20211012153514" checksum="8dee3846e4ca1a0ad4169cf5e4859bcf52b878af" filename="jars/parsington-1.0.1.jar"/>
183 ...

185 ```
186 
187 A few things to notice here:
188 
189 1. By and large, the generator is populating all the right information from the POM sources. Woot!
190 
191 2. If you think about how this must work, the tool needs to resolve every POM and JAR file backward throughout history for that Maven project, which for `net.imagej:imagej` and `sc.fiji:fiji` is a whole lot of files! I'm not sure exactly how many yet, but it's on the order of 2<sup>10</sup> artifacts times 2<sup>4</sup> versions each times 2 (POM and JAR) ~= 2<sup>15</sup> ~= 32K files to download and cache into a `~/.m2/repository` cache. Assuming each JAR files averages 128KB or more, that's at least multiple GB, if not dozens of GB, in total size.
192 
193    To avoid these concerns, the backing `maven.py` library supports *discovery of Maven artifacts directly from a Nexus 2 directory structure*, so that the `updater.py` generator can be executed on the maven.scijava.org server, and run blazingly fast without needing to make any/many remote HTTPS requests. This feature is already testing and working!
194 
195 3. The generator does not bother with `timestamp-obsolete` fields, because this mechanism obviates the need for them. That field was originally added aspirationally with the idea that it could facilitate a future "downgrade" feature for the Updater. But that feature was never coded, and now, we can instead handle downgrading a different way: by generating a *different update site* for each version of a Maven project. For example, https://sites.imagej.net/org.scijava/scijava-common/db-2.94.2.xml.gz, https://sites.imagej.net/org.scijava/scijava-common/db-2.96.0.xml.gz, etc., one `db.xml` file per version of scijava-common. The Updater could then be easily enhanced to switch the update site target for scijava-common to whichever version the user selects from a dropdown box. And https://sites.imagej.net/org.scijava/scijava-common/latest/ can be a rolling symlink to whatever version the maintainers want to be used with the "latest and greatest" ImageJ2, Fiji, etc.
196 
197 4. A consequence of this upgrade/downgrade scheme is that every `db.xml` should include *all versions* of all files, with the *current* version being the desired one. In this way, downgrading from newer versions "just works" without showing any files as "locally modified." But then every time a new project version is released, *all* `db.xml`s must be updated to include it. So it is critical that we get this automation right, so that no manual tweaking is necessary as the number of releases grows for each project.
198 
199 5. The checksum fields are currently wrong: just a hardcoded placeholder. This is because the ImageJ Updater's checksum is a "smart" (and therefore more nuanced to compute) checksum based on the actual contents of the JAR file, rather than the file in total. So even though Maven records MD5 and SHA1 checksums for every artifact, we cannot use these here, and instead will need to compute Updater-style checksums, either in Python or by leaning on the existing Java Updater code. I have not yet investigated doing this, but am confident it can be done in an extensible way. It's just one more piece of work to do for this project.
200 
201 6. There is currently a significant bug/limitation in `maven.py` when properties are used for dependency fields other than `<version>`. For example, some projects will use `<groupId>${project.version}</groupId>` when depending on other components of the same software suite, such as miglayout-swing depending on miglayout-core. The `maven.py` property interpolator currently only evalutes such property expressions in the `<version>` field, not the `<groupId>`, `<artifactId>`, or `<classifier>` fields. You might think it would be straightforward to do so, but the `maven.py` model builder relies on using GACT (groupId/artifactId/classifier/type) tuples as keys in its dependency dicts, because you don't want to include more than one version of the same GACT in the dependency list. But when `${...}` expressions get evaluated in the G/A/C/T fields, it will *change that key value*, which complicates management of those dicts. A) What if we interpolate some such expressions and then there is a new key clash in the dicts? What should we do? B) What if there is a key clash around a G value of `${project.groupId}` which will later evaporate after interpolation? How can we avoid erroneously stomping actually-not-the-same dependencies that temporarily overlap due to identical such expressions? How does maven-core even deal with this? Does it have bugs around such edge cases? Or does it have some hacky logic to avoid these issues? I haven't read the code yet to find out...
202 
203 7. There is some scaffolding in place for OS/arch-specific activation of Maven profiles. But more thought is needed around exactly how to manage this. The typical use case for these is to have OS/arch-specific dependencies, particularly native classifier JARs. It would be nice if the db.xml generation did so for *all* supported platforms, by simulating each active platform when activating profiles, and including such contingent dependencies with the proper `<platform>` declaration in the db.xml. This is doable, but a little tricky to get exactly right for all cases we need to support.
204 
205 ## What's left to do?
206 
207 ### Remaining tasks for this codebase
208 
209 Hopefully the writeup above makes it clear that this project is close to being usable in production for our community. But there are still things left to do before it can really start being tested against real-world use cases.
210 
211 1. Compute and populate the checksums correctly (point 5 of `updater.py` above).
212 
213 2. Fix the bug with test-scoped dependencies being erroneously included (point 6 of `updater.py` above).
214 
215 3. Fix the bug with dependency property interpolation (point 6 of `updater.py` above).
216 
217 4. Implement the platform-specific profiles support (point 7 of `updater.py` above).
218 
219 5. Do more manual generation attempts for `net.imagej:imagej` and `sc.fiji:fiji` (since these two projects will be the eventual inputs for this scheme), fixing any other problems discovered during testing, beyond those already outlined above.
220 
221 6. Enhance the `updater.py` to generate not only *one* update site for a given version of a project, but rather *all* update sites, one per version (points 3 and 4 above).
222 
223 7. Add a way to supplement known versions of each particular project artifact with all non-Maven versions that have historically shipped on core update sites. This is necessary because over the years, many people have built their own local JAR binaries and uploaded them directly, rather than just uploading the official CI builds that were deployed to remote Maven repositories. Fortunately, I think solving this might be as simple as pasting all such historical `previous-versions` into the `template.xml` that contains the scaffolding/boilerplate for update sites in general. Probably only small tweaks needed to `updater.py` to reuse an existing `<plugin>` entry when it's already present from the template itself.
224 
225 8. Consider whether to support "unstable" update sites by tracking SNAPSHOT versions built by CI from latest mainline branches. Support for stable vs unstable update sites has been requested by power users and devs for a long time, and this work could be a good opportunity to deliver on it.
226 
227 ### Tasks in the ImageJ Updater
228 
229 Once the Maven-to-`db.xml` generator is robust enough, we then need to enhance the ImageJ Updater to take advantage of it:
230 
231 1. As described above, the Maven-driven generator will make *one db.xml.gz per release version*.
232 
233 2. To support smoother upgrading of major Java versions over time, the `db.xml` schema must gain a new "minimum Java version" field, which will enable the Updater to know, for every version of a project/site, what version of Java is needed in order to use it. The Maven-to-`db.xml` generator can be enhanced quite easily to embed this minimum Java version into the `db.xml` files it generates.
234 
235 3. The Updater needs to check the currently running version of Java, and refuse to update if it is older than the needed minimum for the currently enabled update sites.
236 
237 4. When a particular project updates to a new major Java version, symlinks can be used to keep track of which versions correspond to which major Java versions. For example, suppose that we now were to release scijava-common at 3.0.0 and require Java 11, making 2.96.0 the last version to support Java 8. The update site `/org.scijava/scijava-common/3.0.0/db.xml.gz` would document Java 11 as minimum, while `/org.scijava/scijava-common/2.96.0/db.xml.gz` would document Java 8 as minimum. The update sites `/org.scijava/scijava-common/latest` and `/org.scijava/scijava-common/latest11` would symlink to `3.0.0`, while the update site `/org.scijava/scijava-common/latest8` would symlink to `2.96.0`. The Updater could then be taught to understand these conventions, and automatically use the right `latest<X>` site for the current major version of Java. In this way, the generic `/latest` symlink might not even be necessary, only the Java-versioned `/latest<X>` links.
238 
239 5. Make the imagej-updater stand alone with no dependencies. In this way, we can have a truly core *Updater* update site (maybe even have the "update the updater" mechanic work specially, outside of the update site infrastructure?) shipping only the latest Java-6-based ImageJ Updater and nothing else, which is common to all ImageJ2 installations. The current imagej-updater only lightly depends on scijava-common (in particular for the uploader plugins, which pragmatically don't actually need to be plugins), so this task is worth doing because it's low effort and avoids the difficult maintenance headache of scijava-common updates breaking ImageJ2 installations by breaking the Updater. We could make the ImageJ Launcher fall back to an graphical installation repair tool in cases where the SciJava Context creation barfs, eliminating this historical source of pain from our user community. In a nutshell: this design gives us the ability to fix bugs in the Updater independently of everything else, which is sorely needed.
240 
241 6. We could split out the original ImageJ's `ij.jar` into its own `ImageJ` update site, so that one can have a barebones ImageJ installation without parts of ImageJ2 beyond only the ImageJ Updater. The Launcher would just need to be smart enough to notice that only ImageJ, not ImageJ2, is currently installed, and start up the application appropriately.
242 
243 7. Relatedly: after tackling the previous two points above, we could probably drop the hard imagej-updater dependency from net.imagej:imagej, instead making it optional.
244 
245 8. Consider teaching the Updater to understand MD5 and/or SHA1 checksums, in addition to its current checksum format. This might simplify generation of update sites. But further investigation needed before deciding. For example: consider how this feature would behave if upgrading from a pre-2023 installation to a post-2023 one.
246 
247 9. Less urgently, hopefully some time in 2024: teach the Updater how to offer "downgrades" using these spiffy new versioned update sites.
248 
249 ### Other tasks
250 
251 We should grow the existing `template.xml` to include all versions currently in https://sites.imagej.net/Java-8/db.xml.gz. It's a big chunk of XML, but size of these `db.xml` files is still tiny compared to the binaries, and it will allow the Updater to easily do awesome things like "convert my Fiji installation into a basic ImageJ2 one" without hassle. I.e. we will get the future split of ImageJ2 core "for free" via generation of `net.imagej:imagej` sites on top of the big honkin' template filled with these historical version hashes.
252 
253 ## Parting thoughts
254 
255 After reading all of the above, you might be thinking: "Gosh, all of this is so complicated! Wouldn't it be easier to just use, e.g., git for versioning and updates? Or adapt some existing Java library that handles it for us?" I just want to ask you to push back on such thoughts: please realize that the above goes into a lot of detail because this scheme will *actually work* while preserving full backwards compatibility, and solve all of our requirements such as upgrading major Java versions cleanly, among many other concerns. The above is really an exhaustive list of all the issues that have occurred to me so far, rather than only vague hand-waving. Whereas on the flip side: while developers in our community have attempted to redesign the ImageJ Updater mechanism wholesale in the past&mdash;and it's relatively easy to get simple proof-of-concept code working e.g. backed by git&mdash;there are many remaining issues with those approaches that were not solved and are potentially hard to solve. I firmly believe that there is no simpler "silver bullet" solution that would obviate the need to do any of the above work. On the contrary: I think the above work is quite minimal compared to any other proposed more radical approach, any of which would have much more substantial wrinkles, difficulties, unintended consequences, etc.
256 
257 Anyway, thank you so much for reading this far! :cookie:
258

52  /**
53   * This class represents a file handled by the updater.
54   * <p>
55   * The ImageJ updater knows about certain files (see
56   * {@link Checksummer#directories} for details). These files can be local-only,
57   * up-to-date, updateable, etc.
58   * </p>
59   * 
60   * @author Johannes Schindelin
61   */
62  public class FileObject {
63  
64      public static class Version implements Comparable<Version> {
65  
66          public String checksum;
67          // This timestamp is not a Unix epoch!
68          // Instead, it is Long.parseLong(Util.timestamp(epoch))
69          public long timestamp;
70          public long timestampObsolete;
71  
72          // optional (can differ from FileObject.filename if the version differs)
73          public String filename;
74  
75          Version(final String checksum, final long timestamp) {
76              this.checksum = checksum;
77              this.timestamp = timestamp;
78          }
79  
80          public Version(Version other) {
81              this.checksum = other.checksum;
82              this.timestamp = other.timestamp;
83              this.filename = other.filename;
84              this.timestampObsolete = other.timestampObsolete;
85          }
86  
87          @Override
88          public int compareTo(final Version other) {
89              final long diff = timestamp - other.timestamp;
90              if (diff != 0) return diff < 0 ? -1 : +1;
91              return checksum.compareTo(other.checksum);
92          }
93  
94          @Override
95          public boolean equals(final Object other) {
96              return other instanceof Version && equals((Version) other);
97          }
98  
99          public boolean equals(final Version other) {
100             return timestamp == other.timestamp && checksum.equals(other.checksum);
101         }
102 
103         @Override
104         public int hashCode() {
105             return (checksum == null ? 0 : checksum.hashCode()) ^
106                 new Long(timestamp).hashCode();
107         }
108 
109         @Override
110         public String toString() {
111             return "Version(" + checksum + ";" + timestamp + ")";
112         }
113     }
114 
115     public enum Action {
116         // no changes
117         LOCAL_ONLY("Local-only"), NOT_INSTALLED("Not installed"), INSTALLED(
118                 "Up-to-date"), UPDATEABLE("Update available"), MODIFIED(
119                 "Locally modified"), NEW("New file"), OBSOLETE("Obsolete"),
120 
121         // changes
122         UNINSTALL("Uninstall it"), INSTALL("Install it"), UPDATE("Update it"),
123 
124         // developer-only changes
125         UPLOAD("Upload it"), REMOVE("Remove it");
126 
127         private final String label;
128 
129         Action(final String label) {
130             this.label = label;
131         }
132 
133         @Override
134         public String toString() {
135             return label;
136         }
137     }
138 
139     public enum Status {
140         NOT_INSTALLED(Action.NOT_INSTALLED, Action.INSTALL, Action.REMOVE),
141         INSTALLED(Action.INSTALLED, Action.UNINSTALL),
142         UPDATEABLE(Action.UPDATEABLE, Action.UNINSTALL, Action.UPDATE, Action.UPLOAD),
143         MODIFIED(Action.MODIFIED, Action.UNINSTALL, Action.UPDATE, Action.UPLOAD),
144         LOCAL_ONLY(Action.LOCAL_ONLY, Action.UNINSTALL, Action.UPLOAD),
145         NEW(Action.NEW, Action.INSTALL, Action.REMOVE),
146         OBSOLETE_UNINSTALLED( Action.NOT_INSTALLED),
147         OBSOLETE(Action.OBSOLETE, Action.UNINSTALL, Action.UPLOAD),
148         OBSOLETE_MODIFIED( Action.MODIFIED, Action.UNINSTALL, Action.UPLOAD);
149 
150         private final Action[] actions;

23  def checksum(path: Path):
24      # CTR FIXME: Calculate the Updater's crazy checksum!
25      return "8dee3846e4ca1a0ad4169cf5e4859bcf52b878af"
26  
27  
28  def timestamp(path: Path) -> str:
29      """Get a timestamp string of the form YYYYMMDDhhmmss."""
30      mtime = path.stat().st_mtime
31      dt = datetime.fromtimestamp(mtime)
32      return dt.strftime("%Y%m%d%H%M%S")
33  
34  
35  def deduce_platform(classifier: str) -> Optional[str]:
36      if "mac" in classifier:
37          return "macosx"
38  
39      if "win" in classifier:
40          platform = "win"
41      elif "linux" in classifier:
42          platform = "linux"
43      else:
44          # Unknown operating system family.
45          return None
46  
47      if any(arch in classifier for arch in ("amd64", "x86_64")):
48          platform += "64"
49      elif any(arch in classifier for arch in ("x86", "i586")):
50          platform += "32"
51      else:
52          # Unknown architecture.
53          return None
54  
55      return platform
56  
57  
58  # -- Classes --
59  
60  class FilesCollection:
61      """
62      TODO
63      """
64  
65      def __init__(self):
66          # Set of artifacts (both current and previous) included in the db.xml.
67          self.artifacts: Set[Artifact] = set()
68          # Set of *current* artifacts included in the db.xml.
69          self.current: Set[Artifact] = set()
70          # Components whose dependencies have already been processed.
71          self.components: Set[Component] = set()
72  
73      def add_artifact(self, artifact: Artifact, current_version: bool = True):
74          # Register artifact.
75          if not self._register_artifact(artifact, current_version):
76              # Artifact already processed.
77              return
78  
79          # Register dependencies.
80          self._register_dependencies(artifact, current_version)
81  
82          # Register other/"previous" versions of the artifact.
83          for component in artifact.component.project.versions():
84              previous_artifact = component.artifact(classifier=artifact.classifier, packaging=artifact.packaging)
85              self.add_artifact(previous_artifact, False)
86  
87      def _register_artifact(self, artifact: Artifact, current_version: bool) -> bool:
88          if artifact in self.artifacts:
89              # Artifact already processed.
90              return False
91          self.artifacts.add(artifact)
92          print(f"Registered {artifact}{' <-- CURRENT' if current_version else ''}")
93          if current_version:
94              self.current.add(artifact)
95          return True
96  
97      def _register_dependencies(self, artifact: Artifact, current_version: bool) -> None:
98          if artifact.component in self.components:
99              # This component's dependencies have already been processed.
100             return
101         self.components.add(artifact.component)
102         model = Model(artifact.component.pom())
103         for dep in model.dependencies():
104             if dep.scope not in ("compile", "runtime"): continue
105             self._register_artifact(dep.artifact, current_version)
106 
107     def generate_xml(self, template_path: Union[Path, str]) -> str:
108         # NB: The remove_blank_text=True option is needed to pretty-print the XML output later:
109         # https://lxml.de/FAQ.html#why-doesn-t-the-pretty-print-option-reformat-my-xml-output
110         with open(template_path) as f:
111             tree = etree.parse(f, parser=etree.XMLParser(remove_blank_text=True))
112 
113         # In Updater terms, a <plugin> entry is a (G, A, C, P) tuple at all versions.
114         # Therefore, we make a list of artifacts (i.e. versions) for each GACP.
115         plugins: Dict[Plugin, List[Artifact]] = {}
116         for artifact in self.artifacts:
117             plugin: Plugin = (artifact.groupId, artifact.artifactId, artifact.classifier, artifact.packaging)
118             if plugin not in plugins:
119                 plugins[plugin] = []
120             plugins[plugin].append(artifact)
121 
122         # Now that we have our list of plugins, we can generate the corresponding XML elements.
123         # CTR TODO: sort by artifactId? Or by groupId/artifactId? Or by something else?
124         for _, artifacts in plugins.items():
125             self._populate_plugin(tree, artifacts)
126 
127         return etree.tostring(tree, xml_declaration=True, pretty_print=True, encoding="utf-8").decode()
128 
129     def _populate_plugin(self, tree, artifacts: List[Artifact]) -> None:
130         # Discern which version is the current one.
131         current_artifacts = [a for a in artifacts if a in self.current]
132         assert len(current_artifacts) <= 1
133         current_artifact = current_artifacts[0] if current_artifacts else None
134         default_artifact = current_artifact or artifacts[0]
135 
136         # <plugin> tag
137         plugin = etree.SubElement(tree.getroot(), "plugin")
138         plugin.set("filename", default_artifact.filename)
139 
140         # <platform> tag
141         platform_string = deduce_platform(default_artifact.classifier)
142         if platform_string:
143             platform = etree.SubElement(plugin, "platform")
144             platform.text = platform_string
145 
146         if current_artifact:
147             # <version> tag
148             version = etree.SubElement(plugin, "version")
149             artifact_path = current_artifact.resolve()
150             version.set("checksum", checksum(artifact_path))
151             version.set("timestamp", timestamp(artifact_path))
152             version.set("filesize", str(artifact_path.stat().st_size))
153 
154             # <description> tag
155             pom = current_artifact.component.pom()
156             desc = pom.description
157             if desc:
158                 description = etree.SubElement(version, "description")
159                 description.text = desc
160 
161             # <dependency> tags
162             model = Model(pom)
163             for dep in model.dependencies():
164                 if dep.scope not in ("compile", "runtime"): continue
165                 dependency = etree.SubElement(version, "dependency")
166                 dependency.set("filename", f"jars/{dep.artifact.filename}")
167                 dependency.set("timestamp", timestamp(dep.artifact.resolve()))
168 
169             # <author> tags
170             # Use developers and contributors from the POM, founders first, then others.
171             people = pom.developers + pom.contributors
172             founders = [p for p in people if "founder" in p.get("roles", [])]
173             others = [p for p in people if p not in founders]
174             for person in founders + others:
175                 name = person.get("name", person.get("id", None))
176                 author = etree.SubElement(version, "author")
177                 author.text = name
178 
179         # <previous-version> tags
180         for artifact in artifacts:
181             if artifact == current_artifact:
182                 # Not a "previous" version!
183                 continue
184             previous_version = etree.SubElement(plugin, "previous-version")
185             artifact_path = artifact.resolve()
186             previous_version.set("timestamp", timestamp(artifact_path))
187             # previous_version.set("timestamp-obsolete", "0")
188             previous_version.set("checksum", checksum(artifact_path))
189             previous_version.set("filename", f"jars/{artifact.filename}")
190 
191 
192 # -- Main --
193 
194 def main(args):
195     debug = bool(os.environ.get("DEBUG", None))
196     log_format = "[%(levelname)s] %(message)s"
197     log_level = logging.DEBUG if debug else logging.INFO
198     logging.basicConfig(format=log_format, level=log_level)
199 
200     # Create appropriate Maven environment.
201     storage = Path("/opt/sonatype-work/nexus/storage")
202     release_repos = ["releases", "thirdparty", "sonatype", "sonatype-s01", "central", "ome-releases"]
203     snapshot_repos = ["snapshots", "sonatype-snapshots", "sonatype-snapshots-s01", "ome-snapshots"]
204     local_repos = [repo for r in release_repos + snapshot_repos if (repo := storage / r).exists()]
205     remote_repos = {
206         "scijava.public": "https://maven.scijava.org/content/groups/public"
207     }
208     print("Creating Maven environment...")
209     env = Environment(local_repos=local_repos, remote_repos=remote_repos)
210     #def fail_download(*args):
211     #    raise RuntimeError(f"Should not be downloading: {args[0]}")
212     #env.resolver.download = fail_download
213 
214     print("Initializing FilesCollection...")
215     fc = FilesCollection()
216 
217     # Process arguments.
218     coords = [arg for arg in args if ":" in arg]
219     for coord in coords:
220         print(f"Processing {coord}...")
221         tokens = coord.split(":")
222         g = tokens[0]
223         a = tokens[1]
224         project = env.project(g, a)
225         v = tokens[2] if len(tokens) > 2 else project.release
226         component = project.at_version(v)
227         artifact = component.artifact()
228         print(f"Adding artifact {artifact}...")
229         fc.add_artifact(artifact)
230 
231     print("Generating resultant XML...")
232     xml = fc.generate_xml("template.xml")
233     with open(f"db-{g}-{a}-{v}.xml", "w") as f:
234         f.write(xml)
235 
236 
237 if __name__ == "__main__":
238     main(sys.argv[1:])

52  /**
53   * A helper class to read information from Maven-built .jar files.
54   * 
55   * @author Johannes Schindelin
56   */
57  public class POMParser extends DefaultHandler {
58      private FileObject file;
59      private String body, prefix;
60  
61      public POMParser(final FileObject file) {
62          this.file = file;
63      }
64  
65      public static void fillMetadataFromJar(final FileObject object, final File file) throws ParserConfigurationException, IOException, SAXException {
66          final JarFile jar = new JarFile(file);
67          for (final JarEntry entry : UpdaterUtil.iterate(jar.entries())) {
68              if (entry.getName().matches("META-INF/maven/.*/pom.xml")) {
69                  new POMParser(object).read(jar.getInputStream(entry));
70                  break;
71              }
72          }
73          jar.close();
74      }
75  
76      public void read(final InputStream in) throws ParserConfigurationException, IOException, SAXException {
77          final InputSource inputSource = new InputSource(in);
78          final SAXParserFactory factory = SAXParserFactory.newInstance();
79          factory.setNamespaceAware(true);
80  
81          // commented-out as per Postel's law
82          // factory.setValidating(true);
83  
84          final SAXParser parser = factory.newSAXParser();
85          final XMLReader xr = parser.getXMLReader();
86          xr.setContentHandler(this);
87          xr.setErrorHandler(new XMLFileErrorHandler());
88          xr.parse(inputSource);
89      }
90  
91      @Override
92      public void startDocument() {
93          body = "";
94          prefix = "";
95      }
96  
97      @Override
98      public void endDocument() {}
99  
100     @Override
101     public void startElement(final String uri, final String name,
102         final String qName, final Attributes atts)
103     {
104         prefix += ">" + qName;
105         body = "";
106     }
107 
108     @Override
109     public void
110         endElement(final String uri, final String name, final String qName)
111     {
112         if (prefix.equals(">project>description")) {
113             if (!"".equals(body)) {
114                 file.description = body;
115                 file.descriptionFromPOM = true;
116             }
117         }
118         else if (prefix.equals(">project>developers>developer>name")) {
119             file.addAuthor(body);
120         }
121 
122         prefix = prefix.substring(0, prefix.length() - 1 - qName.length());
123     }
124 
125     @Override
126     public void characters(final char ch[], final int start, final int length) {
127         body += new String(ch, start, length);
128     }

Bash Read Fiji's populate-app and Maven upload scripts 
head -60 /home/curtis/code/fiji/fiji/bin/populate-app.sh; echo "==="; head -50 /home/curtis/code/fiji/fiji/bin/upload-plugin-from-maven.sh 2>/dev/null; echo "==="; grep -n "populate\|updater\|db.xml" /home/curtis/code/fiji/fiji/.github/workflows/build.yml | head
#!/bin/sh

set -e

# Copies the state of this Fiji working copy into the given Fiji.app directory.
# This will ***ERASE ANY EXISTING JAR FILES*** in the given Fiji.app directory!
# This script is intended for updating an existing Fiji.app, so that the new
# state can then be uploaded to the core update site via the ImageJ Updater.

die() { echo "$1"; exit 1; }

test $# -eq 1 || die "Usage: bin/populate-app.sh /path/to/Fiji.app"
dir=$1
test -d "$dir" || die "No such directory: $dir"
dir=$(cd "$1" && pwd)

cd "$(dirname "$0")/.."

set -x

# Remove old libraries.
find "$dir/jars" "$dir/plugins" -type f -name '*.jar' | \
  grep -v '\(STUB\|jars/xalan-\|jars/serializer-\)' | \
  while read jar; do rm "$jar"; done

# Copy over new libraries.
mvn -DskipTests -Denforcer.skip -Dscijava.app.directory="$dir"

# Relocate incorrectly located libraries.
mv -f "$dir/jars/metakit-"*.jar            "$dir/jars/bio-formats/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/ome-codecs-"*.jar         "$dir/jars/bio-formats/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/ome-common-"*.jar         "$dir/jars/bio-formats/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/ome-jai-"*.jar            "$dir/jars/bio-formats/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/ome-mdbtools-"*.jar       "$dir/jars/bio-formats/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/ome-poi-"*.jar            "$dir/jars/bio-formats/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/ome-xml-"*.jar            "$dir/jars/bio-formats/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/specification-"*.jar      "$dir/jars/bio-formats/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/Correct_3D_Drift-"*.jar   "$dir/plugins/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/KymographBuilder-"*.jar   "$dir/plugins/"
mv -f "$dir/jars/bigdataviewer_fiji-"*.jar "$dir/plugins/"

# HACK: Install libraries with clashing artifactIds.
# org.antlr:antlr:3.5.2 vs antlr:antlr:2.7.7
mvn -q dependency:copy -DoutputDirectory="$dir/jars" -Dartifact=antlr:antlr:2.7.7
mv "$dir/jars/antlr-2.7.7.jar" "$dir/jars/antlr.antlr-2.7.7.jar"

# Install native libraries for all platforms.
mvn dependency:list |
  grep '\(macosx\|windows\|linux\)-' |
  grep -v '(optional)' |
  sed 's/.* \(.*\):[^:]*$/\1/' | while read native
do
  set +x
  g=${native%%:*}
  r=${native#*:}
  a=${r%%:*}
  r=${r#*:}
  p=${r%%:*}
  r=${r#*:}
  c=${r%%:*}
===
#!/bin/sh

# --- DEPRECATED --
# use upload-plugins-by-gav.sh
# Downloads a plugin's newest snapshot version and uploads it to the
# specified update site.
#
# It expects to find maven-helper.sh in bin/, too

die () {
    echo "$*" >&2
    exit 1
}

echo "WARNING: This script is deprecated. Use upload-plugins-by-gav.sh instead"

test $# = 4 ||
die "Usage: $0 <groupId> <artifactId> <update-site> <webdav-user>"

groupId="$1"
artifactId="$2"
update_site="$3"
webdav_user="$4"
url="http://sites.imagej.net/$update_site/"

maven_helper=bin/maven-helper.sh
version="$(sh $maven_helper latest-version $groupId:$artifactId:SNAPSHOT)"

# determine correct launcher to launch MiniMaven and the Updater
case "$(uname -s),$(uname -m)" in
Linux,x86_64) launcher=ImageJ-linux64;;
Linux,*) launcher=ImageJ-linux32;;
Darwin,*) launcher=Contents/MacOS/ImageJ-tiger;;
MING*,*) launcher=ImageJ-win32.exe;;
*) echo "Unknown platform" >&2; exit 1;;
esac

# initialize Fiji.app/ directory
curl -O http://update.imagej.net/bootstrap.js
jrunscript bootstrap.js update-force-pristine

# delete the artifact, just in case
rm -f "plugins/$artifactId-$version.jar"

# install plugin and dependencies using the imagej-maven-plugin
mvn -Ddelete.other.versions=true -Dforce=true -Dimagej.app.directory=$(pwd) \
    net.imagej:imagej-maven-plugin:0.5.4:install-artifact \
    -Dartifact=$groupId:$artifactId:$version

# upload complete update site

Claude 2026-07-03 21:35:30

I’ve now got a good picture of all three pieces. Here’s what I found, and then my opinion.

What I’m looking at

imagej-updater is fundamentally file-centric: db.xml.gz keys everything by filename, identifies versions by (custom JAR-content checksum, timestamp), expresses dependencies as filenames, and downloads from site.getURL() + filename + "-" + timestamp (FilesCollection.java:941). Its only Maven awareness is POMParser scraping description/authors out of the embedded pom.xml. The custom checksum (UpdaterUtil.getJarDigest) deliberately normalizes manifests and properties files so independently-rebuilt-but-equivalent JARs hash the same — that exists because of a decade of hand-built uploads.

db-xml-maven is the opposite philosophy applied server-side: maven.py is a ~1300-line pure-Python Maven model/resolver, and updater.py walks a G:A:V plus its full version history and emits an ordinary db.xml. Per its README it’s ~90% there, with four known holes: real checksums (currently a hardcoded placeholder), property interpolation in G/A/C fields, platform-profile activation, and the historical non-Maven versions backfill. It’s been dormant since September 2023 (the November 2025 commit was just removing an .iml file).

fiji/fiji’s pom.xml is already, de facto, the bill of materials for the core update site — 161 direct dependencies, versions pinned via pom-scijava 45. But the pipeline from POM to update site is populate-app.sh (with its hand-maintained mv hacks for misfiled JARs) plus a human running the Updater in developer mode. That manual hop is where drift and errors come from.

My opinion: db-xml-maven has the right architecture — but probably the wrong implementation language

The question “complete db-xml-maven vs. teach the Updater Maven coordinates” is really “where should Maven resolution happen: publish time or update time?” I think publish time wins decisively, and here’s the core reason: db.xml is a lockfile. It’s a fully-resolved, checksummed snapshot of an application state. That is exactly the right thing for a client to consume — it’s how pip, apt, and conda all work; no mainstream package manager makes every client re-run dependency resolution that can be done once at release time. Client-side Maven semantics would move the hardest, most edge-case-riddled logic (POM inheritance, profiles, property interpolation, import scope, exclusions) into the one component where bugs are catastrophic: the self-updating bootstrap path that historically bricks installations when it breaks.

The other arguments against Maven-coordinates-in-the-client compound that:

  • Rollout chicken-and-egg. New db.xml semantics require clients that understand them, but clients acquire new Updaters through update sites. Server-side generation emits plain old db.xml that every deployed client from the last 15 years already understands. That’s an enormous asymmetry.
  • A Maven resolver in the client conflicts with your own goal (README task #5) of making imagej-updater standalone and dependency-free. Maven Resolver plus its transitive baggage is the opposite of that; a hand-rolled minimal resolver is the same edge-case swamp maven.py is stuck in, relocated to the worst possible place.
  • Update sites will never be Maven-only. Scripts, macros, LUTs, launchers, bundled Javas, sample images, native libs — plus hundreds of third-party sites where people upload hand-built JARs through the GUI. The file-based schema has to survive regardless, so client-side Maven support buys a hybrid schema forever rather than a simplification.

So: complete the db-xml-maven mission. But before finishing the Python prototype, I’d seriously consider rewriting the generator in Java, as a module in or beside imagej-updater, for three reasons that each eliminate one of the prototype’s remaining hard problems:

  1. Checksums for free. Task #1 (the “crazy checksum”) is a direct call to UpdaterUtil.getJarDigest() — no reimplementation, no risk of subtle divergence that shows every file as “locally modified” for every user.
  2. Exact Maven semantics for free. Tasks #3 and part of #6 (property interpolation, model building) disappear if you use the real maven-resolver / maven-model-builder libraries — you get maven-core’s actual behavior, including the edge cases the README agonizes over (“How does maven-core even deal with this?” — you don’t have to know). The generator runs in CI or on the server, never ships to users, so dependency weight is irrelevant.
  3. Platform profiles become tractable. Task #4 (OS/arch profile activation) becomes “resolve N times with injected os.name/os.arch properties, diff the results, emit <platform> tags” — using Maven’s own activation logic rather than simulating it.

What survives from db-xml-maven unchanged is everything that was actually novel and right: the one-db.xml-per-release-version layout, the all-versions-as-previous-version design for clean up/downgrades, the template of historical non-Maven hashes, and the min-Java-version convention (which just got urgent with the Java 21 move). Those are schema and site-layout decisions, independent of generator language. And maven.py still has real value for jgo/status.scijava.org/javadoc-wrangler — that migration is worth doing on its own merits; it just shouldn’t be load-bearing for update-site integrity.

Where Maven coordinates do belong in db.xml

There’s a cheap middle path I’d fold in: have the generator stamp an informational coordinate="G:A:V" attribute on generated <plugin>/<version> entries. Old clients ignore unknown attributes (XMLFileReader pulls attributes by name), so it’s fully backward-compatible, and it gives you provenance, future tooling hooks, and eventually the option of a client that prefers downloading from maven.scijava.org. Relatedly, you don’t need client changes to stop duplicating JARs onto sites.imagej.net: the site server can answer the existing filename-timestamp URL pattern with HTTP redirects into Nexus storage. Both are additive; neither requires the risky client rewrite.

Suggested sequence

  1. Java generator module (imagej-updater-maven or a db-xml subpackage): G:A:V → db.xml using maven-resolver + existing UpdaterUtil/XMLFileWriter code. Validate by generating the Fiji core site from sc.fiji:fiji:2.18.x and diffing against reality.
  2. Backfill the historical-versions template from the Java-8 site’s db.xml (README’s “Other tasks” item — mostly mechanical).
  3. Wire into CI: releasing sc.fiji:fiji regenerates and publishes the site, retiring populate-app.sh + manual upload.
  4. Then the small client-side increments in whatever order pain dictates: min-Java field, versioned-site switching, standalone updater. Each is independent and each ships to a world where the server side already works.

The one scenario where I’d reconsider is if you want third-party developers to get update sites “for free” by just doing Maven releases — but even that is better served by offering them the generator (a CI action: “point it at your G:A”) than by teaching every user’s client to resolve POMs.

You 2026-07-03 22:22:04

Great analysis. I fully understand why you suggest a Java rewrite – it’s very logical to avoid implementation skew! However, as of 2026 now, the jgo library overhaul from v1 to v2 is complete, and successfully rolled in the maven.py work from this project and beyond. This work is browseable at ~/code/apposed/jgo, and is now mature and released and does Maven dependency resolution as well (actually better, IMHO) than mvn does. There is also a BOM management tool called pombast, browseable at ~/code/scijava/pombast, for pom-scijava management and testing, and status.scijava.org generation and maintenance. All of this is working well in production now. We needed this functionality in Python for better Java+Python integration in a variety of circumstances.

You said: > no mainstream package manager makes every client re-run dependency resolution > that can be done once at release time.

But Maven does sort of work that way: for a given Java library at a given release tag, checking out that tag does not come with a lockfile, because Maven doesn’t need one, because the resolution is deterministic and reproducible as long as snapshots and version ranges weren’t used. This is a huge advantage of Maven’s dependency resolution model compared to most other mainstream package managers. While I do still think Java projects would benefit from shipping a BOM/manifest/lockfile-style list of resolved dependencies, there is no standard in the Java world that I know of; the closest is JAR manifests having a Class-Path entry with flattened list of JAR files, although it’s imperfect at best.

I do agree with you that it feels wrong to have the ImageJ Updater do dependency re-resolution on every update… until you think about composition of extensions/plugins/update-sites. Maven is unusual in that it achieves reproducibility with composability. With a lockfile, you lose composability; without one, you lose reproducibility (with many package managers). If installed plugins are Maven coordinates, we could then resolve the dependency graph and install the composed set of extensions accordingly, same as Maven would with a Java project depending on those things. I find this design compelling, if we can pull it off.

For the db-xml-maven approach: if we finish it, I’d want to add a jgo dependency and use that. Of the “four known holes” you noted, two of them – property interpolation in G/A/C fields and platform-profile activation – are both implemented in jgo now. So is the more robust maven-metadata.xml remote querying and aggregation touched on in the db-xml-maven README. That leaves only “checksums” and “historical non-Maven versions backfill” – really, the “checksums” part is the main thing left to do, and it’s not super difficult.

How do concepts from the two paradigms align here? - On the ImageJ/Fiji side, we have update sites consisting of files which can be (but are not necessarily) JAR files and which can have other files as dependencies. A given Fiji installation often has multiple update sites enabled with multiple files each. - On the Maven side, we have projects (G:A) that are materialized at versions as components (G:A:V) consisting of artifacts (G:A:V:C:P) with direct and transitive dependencies.

As db-xml-maven is currently designed, it generates a different update site for each V of a project, as described in points (3) and (4) of the db-xml-maven README’s “few things to notice” list.

This might be OK, but I’m worried about composing multiple update sites. Maven’s “nearest wins” rule of dependency graphs does not result in the same behavior as the Updater’s “later update sites trump earlier ones” behavior.

So with all of that in mind:

  • Should we still finish db-xml-maven?
  • Should we teach the Updater to understand Maven coordinates somehow?
    • If so, what do the schema changes look like? How much dependency pre-resolution should we bake in?

Despite what I wrote in 2023, I feel like maybe it would be sufficient to enhance only the Updater here to “speak Maven” more fluently, without the complexity and rigamarole of db-xml-maven’s pom.xml-to-db.xml.gz step… but we need to think carefully about composition of extensions, including Maven + non-Maven extensions together.

# pombast

[![Build Status](https://github.com/scijava/pombast/actions/workflows/build.yml/badge.svg)](https://github.com/scijava/pombast/actions/workflows/build.yml)

**Validate that Maven Bill-of-Materials (BOM) components actually work together.**

---

## Status & Trajectory

> **Pre-alpha. Not yet suitable for general use.**
>
> Pombast is under active development and breaking changes are expected.
> APIs, CLI options, configuration format, and caching behavior may all
> change without notice until a stable 1.0 release.
>
> **Intended trajectory:** Pombast is being built as a principled replacement
> for the [pom-scijava](https://github.com/scijava/pom-scijava) *mega-melt*,
> the ad-hoc shell-script process that validates managed components before
> each pom-scijava release. The goal is to make BOM validation reproducible,
> configurable, and automatable outside of any single repository's CI
> infrastructure, and usable for any Maven BOM, not just SciJava's.

---

## What it does

Pombast provides two independent validation modes:

### `pombast melt` — holistic classpath check

Generates a single "mega-melt" POM that lists every BOM component as a direct
dependency, inheriting versions from the BOM's own `<dependencyManagement>`, and
runs Maven against it. Catches duplicate classes across the full BOM classpath
and SNAPSHOT dependencies without touching individual component source trees.

### `pombast smelt` — per-component build and test

For each filtered component:

1. Resolves source code via SCM metadata in the POM.
2. Rewrites the component POM to pin every dependency to the BOM-declared version.
3. Optionally tests binary compatibility of the already-published JAR against the
   pinned dependency set (catches runtime breakage without rebuilding from source).
4. Rebuilds from source and runs the test suite.
5. Reports success, failure, or error with timing and per-component build logs.

Both commands are independent and can be run separately or together in CI.

---

# jgo: painless Java environments and execution

[![License](https://img.shields.io/pypi/l/jgo.svg)](https://github.com/apposed/jgo/raw/main/UNLICENSE)
[![PyPI](https://img.shields.io/pypi/v/jgo.svg)](https://pypi.org/project/jgo)
[![Python Version](https://img.shields.io/pypi/pyversions/jgo.svg)](https://python.org)
[![Build Status](https://github.com/apposed/jgo/actions/workflows/build.yml/badge.svg)](https://github.com/apposed/jgo/actions/workflows/build.yml)
[![Documentation](https://readthedocs.org/projects/jgo/badge/?version=latest)](https://jgo.apposed.org/)

![](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apposed/jgo/main/jgo.svg)

## Summary

`jgo` launches Java applications directly from Maven coordinates—no installation required. Specify a Maven artifact identifier and an optional main class, and `jgo` resolves dependencies, materializes the environment including the version of Java needed, and runs your program.

```bash
# Run Jython REPL (latest version)
jgo org.python:jython-standalone

# Run with specific version
jgo org.python:jython-standalone:2.7.3
```

### Features

- **Zero-configuration execution**: Automatic Java download and version management thanks to `cjdk` integration
- **Pure Python dependency resolver**: No Maven installation required to resolve dependency graphs
- **Reproducible environments**: `jgo.toml` project files with lock files (like `package.json` + `package-lock.json`)
- **Three-layer architecture**: Independently useful layers for Maven resolution, environment building, and execution
- **Powerful Python API**: Fine-grained control over dependency resolution and execution
- **Intelligent module handling**: Full support of the Java module system (JPMS), including
  intelligent classification of dependencies on module-path or class-path as appropriate

## Quick Start

### CLI Usage

```bash
# Run Jython REPL
jgo org.python:jython-standalone

# Run with arguments
jgo org.python:jython-standalone -- -- script.py --verbose

# Multiple artifacts with main class (use @ separator)
jgo org.scijava:scijava-common+org.scijava:scripting-jython@ScriptREPL

# Force update from remote repos
jgo -u org.python:jython-standalone

# Use specific Java version
jgo --java-version 17 net.imagej:imagej

# Print classpath without running
jgo info classpath org.python:jython-standalone

# Preview commands without executing (--dry-run)
jgo --dry-run run org.scijava:parsington         # See java command
jgo --dry-run init org.python:jython-standalone  # Preview jgo.toml
```

### Python API

```python
import jgo

# Simple one-liner
jgo.run("org.python:jython-standalone:2.7.3", app_args=["script.py"])

# Build environment without running
env = jgo.build("org.python:jython-standalone")
print(env.classpath)  # List of JAR paths

# Resolve dependencies
components = jgo.resolve("org.python:jython-standalone")
for comp in components:
    print(f"{comp.groupId}:{comp.artifactId}:{comp.version}")
```
