class PythonResolver(Resolver):
    """
    A resolver that works by pure Python code.
    Low overhead, but less feature complete than mvn.
    """

    def __init__(
        self,
        profile_constraints: ProfileConstraints | None = None,
        progress_callback: ProgressCallback | None = None,
        lenient: bool = False,
    ):
        """
        Initialize Python resolver.

        Args:
            profile_constraints: Profile constraints for Maven model building.
                Any None platform fields (os_name, os_family, os_arch) are
                filled in from the current system, matching Maven's behavior
                of always having OS properties available during resolution.
            progress_callback: Optional callback for download progress reporting.
                Receives (filename, total_size) and returns a context manager
                that yields an update function accepting bytes_count.
            lenient: If True, warn instead of failing on unresolvable dependencies.
        """
        if profile_constraints is None:
            profile_constraints = ProfileConstraints()
        if None in (
            profile_constraints.os_name,
            profile_constraints.os_family,
            profile_constraints.os_arch,
        ):
            os_name, os_family, os_arch = detect_os_properties()
            profile_constraints = dataclasses.replace(
                profile_constraints,
                os_name=profile_constraints.os_name or os_name,
                os_family=profile_constraints.os_family or os_family,
                os_arch=profile_constraints.os_arch or os_arch,
            )
        self.profile_constraints = profile_constraints
        self.progress_callback = progress_callback
        self.lenient = lenient

    def download(self, artifact: Artifact) -> Path | None:
        # For SNAPSHOT versions, ensure we have the metadata first
        is_snapshot = artifact.version.endswith("-SNAPSHOT")
        if is_snapshot:
            # Check if we already have metadata cached
            if not artifact.component.snapshot_metadata:
                # Try to fetch metadata from remote repos
                artifact.component.update_snapshot_metadata()

            # If still no metadata, we might be dealing with a remote repo
            # that doesn't have the artifact yet
            if not artifact.component.snapshot_metadata:
                _log.warning(
                    f"No SNAPSHOT metadata found for {artifact.component}. "
                    "Attempting download with SNAPSHOT version..."
                )

        for remote_repo in artifact.context.remote_repos.values():
            # Convert Path to forward-slash string for URL
            path_str = str(artifact.component.path_prefix).replace("\\", "/")
            url = f"{remote_repo}/{path_str}/{artifact.filename}"
            _log.debug(f"Trying {url}")

            # Use streaming to enable progress bar
            response: requests.Response = http.get(
                url, stream=True, timeout=artifact.context.timeout
            )
            if response.status_code == 200:
                # Remote artifact accessed successfully
                cached_file = artifact.cached_path
                assert cached_file is not None
                assert not cached_file.exists()
                cached_file.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

                # Get total size from Content-Length header
                total_size = int(response.headers.get("content-length", 0))

                # Hash while streaming to avoid re-reading the file for checksum verification
                sha1 = hashlib.sha1()

                # Use progress callback if provided and size is known
                if self.progress_callback and total_size > 0:
                    with self.progress_callback(
                        artifact.filename, total_size
                    ) as update_progress:
                        with open(cached_file, "wb") as f:
                            for chunk in response.iter_content(chunk_size=8192):
                                f.write(chunk)
                                sha1.update(chunk)
                                update_progress(len(chunk))
                else:
                    with open(cached_file, "wb") as f:
                        for chunk in response.iter_content(chunk_size=8192):
                            f.write(chunk)
                            sha1.update(chunk)

                _verify_remote_sha1(
                    url, sha1.hexdigest(), artifact.filename, artifact.context.timeout
                )

                if is_snapshot:
                    _log.info(f"Downloaded SNAPSHOT {artifact} to {cached_file}")
                else:
                    _log.debug(f"Downloaded {artifact} to {cached_file}")
                return cached_file

        raise RuntimeError(
            f"Artifact {artifact} not found in remote repositories "
            f"{artifact.context.remote_repos}"
        )

    def resolve(
        self,
        dependencies: list[Dependency],
        transitive: bool = True,
        optional_depth: int = 0,
    ) -> tuple[list[Dependency], list[Dependency]]:
        """

Claude 2026-07-03 22:24:21

Bash Read jgo resolve mediation algorithm 
sed -n '310,452p' /home/curtis/code/apposed/jgo/src/jgo/maven/_resolver.py
"""
        Get all dependencies for the given input dependencies.

        Args:
            dependencies: List of input dependencies
            transitive: Whether to include transitive dependencies
            optional_depth: Maximum depth at which to include optional dependencies

        Returns:
            Tuple of (resolved_inputs, resolved_transitive) where:
            - resolved_inputs: Input deps with MANAGED versions resolved
            - resolved_transitive: Transitive dependencies (excludes the inputs themselves)
        """
        if not dependencies:
            raise ValueError("At least one dependency is required")

        boms = _compute_boms(dependencies)

        pom = create_pom(dependencies, boms)
        model = Model(
            pom,
            dependencies[0].context,
            profile_constraints=self.profile_constraints,
            lenient=self.lenient,
        )
        # When transitive=False, set max_depth=1 to get one level of dependencies
        # from the synthetic wrapper (i.e., the direct dependencies of the components)
        max_depth = 1 if not transitive else None
        deps, _ = model.dependencies(max_depth=max_depth, optional_depth=optional_depth)

        # Build resolved component dependencies
        resolved_inputs, artifact_keys = _resolve_component_inputs(dependencies, deps)

        # Filter out components from transitive deps list
        resolved_transitive = [
            dep for dep in deps if dep.artifact.key not in artifact_keys
        ]

        # Filter out test scope dependencies (they're not needed for running the application)
        resolved_transitive = [
            dep for dep in resolved_transitive if dep.scope not in ("test",)
        ]

        return resolved_inputs, resolved_transitive

    def get_dependency_list(
        self,
        dependencies: list[Dependency],
        transitive: bool = True,
        optional_depth: int = 0,
    ) -> tuple[DependencyNode, list[DependencyNode]]:
        """
        Get the flat list of resolved dependencies as data structures.

        Args:
            dependencies: List of input dependencies
            transitive: If False, return only direct dependencies
            optional_depth: Maximum depth at which to include optional dependencies

        Returns:
            Tuple of (root_node, flat_list_of_dependencies)
        """
        if not dependencies:
            raise ValueError("At least one dependency is required")

        boms = _compute_boms(dependencies)

        pom = create_pom(dependencies, boms)
        model = Model(
            pom,
            dependencies[0].context,
            profile_constraints=self.profile_constraints,
            lenient=self.lenient,
        )

        # Create resolved input deps list using model.deps for MANAGED versions
        resolved_input_deps = []
        for input_dep in dependencies:
            comp = input_dep.artifact.component
            if comp.version == "MANAGED":
                # Find resolved version in model.deps
                resolved_version = None
                for dep in model.deps.values():
                    if (
                        dep.groupId == comp.groupId
                        and dep.artifactId == comp.artifactId
                    ):
                        resolved_version = dep.version
                        break
                if resolved_version:
                    # Create a new dependency with the resolved version
                    resolved_comp = comp.project.at_version(resolved_version)
                    resolved_dep = Dependency(
                        resolved_comp.artifact(input_dep.classifier, input_dep.type),
                        scope=input_dep.scope,
                        optional=input_dep.optional,
                        exclusions=input_dep.exclusions,
                        raw=input_dep.raw,
                    )
                    resolved_input_deps.append(resolved_dep)
                else:
                    resolved_input_deps.append(input_dep)  # fallback
            else:
                resolved_input_deps.append(input_dep)

        max_depth = 1 if not transitive else None
        deps, _ = model.dependencies(max_depth=max_depth, optional_depth=optional_depth)
        deps = _filter_component_deps(deps, resolved_input_deps)
        deps = [dep for dep in deps if dep.scope not in ("test",)]

        return _build_dependency_list(resolved_input_deps, deps)

    def get_dependency_tree(
        self,
        dependencies: list[Dependency],
        optional_depth: int = 0,
    ) -> DependencyNode:
        """
        Get the full dependency tree as a data structure.

        Args:
            dependencies: List of input dependencies
            optional_depth: Maximum depth at which to include optional dependencies

        Returns:
            Root DependencyNode with full tree structure
        """
        boms = _compute_boms(dependencies)

        # Build model and get dependency tree
        pom = create_pom(dependencies, boms)
        model = Model(
            pom,
            dependencies[0].context,
            profile_constraints=self.profile_constraints,
            lenient=self.lenient,
        )
        _, root = model.dependencies(optional_depth=optional_depth)

        return root


class MvnResolver(Resolver):

Claude 2026-07-03 22:26:36

jgo v2 changes my calculus significantly — and one thing I found in it changes the shape of the answer. Let me lay out what I saw, then answer your questions directly.

The load-bearing discovery: PythonResolver.resolve() takes a list of dependencies, builds a synthetic root POM over them (with BOM support via _compute_boms), and resolves the composed set with Maven semantics — plus injectable ProfileConstraints for cross-platform resolution, SHA1 verification on download, and a lockfile layer. That is exactly the composition primitive you’re describing: “installation = synthetic root project depending on all selected extensions, managed by a BOM.” And I notice pombast melt is the same operation wearing a validation hat — the mega-melt POM is a simulated Fiji installation. So the paradigm you want the Updater to adopt is already implemented twice in your stack; the question is only how it reaches the Java client on user machines.

Conceding and refining the lockfile point

You’re right that Maven’s determinism makes it lockfile-free-with-composability, and that’s the property worth preserving. But I’d refine where the line sits. Maven resolution has two layers:

  1. Model building — POM inheritance, BOM imports, property interpolation, profile activation, exclusions, scope logic. This is where the decade of edge cases lives (it’s most of jgo’s _model.py/_pom.py/_profile.py), and it’s a per-component computation whose output is deterministic facts: “component G:A:V has these direct deps at these versions, per platform.”
  2. Graph mediation — walk the composed graph, nearest-wins, root dependencyManagement overrides. This is the composition-dependent part — and it’s small: BFS with depth tracking plus ComparableVersion ordering.

Layer 1 can be computed once at publish time without losing any composability, because its output doesn’t depend on what it’s composed with. Layer 2 is what must run client-side to get Maven’s reproducibility-with-composability. So the design principle I’d propose: ship the facts, compute the choice. Publish flattened per-component dependency graphs; let the client mediate over the union.

Q1: Should you finish db-xml-maven?

No — not as designed. Both halves are superseded: maven.py became jgo v2, and the “one pre-resolved update site per version” generation scheme was a workaround for a coordinate-ignorant client. You flagged the problem with it yourself, and I agree it’s disqualifying: pre-resolved sites compose by filename shadowing, which is neither Maven’s semantics nor predictable, and the all-versions-in-every-db.xml + regenerate-everything-on-every-release design was the tail wagging the dog. Retire the repo. What survives is its mission and a much smaller artifact: a jgo-based “flattener” that turns G:A:V into published site metadata — maybe 200 lines against jgo’s API, run in CI. The historical-versions template idea survives too, for the legacy bridge.

Q2: Yes, teach the Updater coordinates — here’s the shape

Schema. Coordinate-keyed entries alongside (not replacing) filename-keyed ones. Identity becomes G:A:C:P; version becomes a first-class variable rather than a filename substring:

<component coordinate="sc.fiji:TrackMate" current="7.12.0">
  <release version="7.12.0" sha1="..." filesize="..." timestamp="..." min-java="8">
    <description>...</description>
    <dependency coordinate="net.imglib2:imglib2:6.2.0"/>
    <dependency coordinate="org.jogamp.gluegen:gluegen-rt:2.4.0"
                classifier="natives-linux-amd64" platform="linux64"/>
    <dependency coordinate="com.google.guava:guava:32.1.2-jre">
      <exclude>com.google.code.findbugs:jsr305</exclude>
    </dependency>
  </release>
  <release version="7.11.0" sha1="..." min-java="8">...</release>
</component>

Each <release> carries the component’s direct dependencies with versions as resolved by model building (inheritance, interpolation, profiles pre-expanded per platform — jgo’s ProfileConstraints does N platforms at publish time), plus per-edge exclusions and optional flags. That’s everything mediation needs. Scope filtering happens at publish (compile/runtime only). Note what’s absent: no crazy checksum — Maven artifacts are immutable, so SHA1 suffices for both download verification and local-modification detection; the smart JAR digest’s whole reason for existing (matching independently rebuilt JARs) is an anti-feature for release artifacts.

The core site additionally publishes its BOM:

<managed bom="org.scijava:pom-scijava:45.0.0">
  <version coordinate="net.imglib2:imglib2:6.2.0"/>
  ...
</managed>

Client semantics. The installation is the synthetic root. Every component the user has selected (across all enabled sites) is a depth-1 dependency. Mediation: root-managed version wins (the core BOM — this is what keeps Fiji coherent, and pombast is what keeps it honest), then nearest-wins with Maven’s tie-breaking. This has a genuinely nice property: “what the user explicitly installed” is depth-1, so user intent dominates transitive defaults, which matches both Maven semantics and user expectations. Site order stops mattering for coordinate entries — conflicts are mediated, not shadowed — which fixes exactly the composition mismatch you’re worried about. The union-of-graphs is closed: any version mediation can select was published by some site along with its edge list, so the client never needs a POM.

Client-side cost: one index fetch per site (same as today — no per-POM HTTP storm, which matters for the firewall-afflicted institutions that already struggle with the Updater), then local BFS + ComparableVersion. A few hundred lines of Java. The skew risk I worried about in 2023 shrinks to just this mediation algorithm, and you can pin it down mechanically: have jgo generate golden test vectors (composed inputs → expected selection) and run them in imagej-updater’s CI. jgo becomes the reference implementation rather than a parallel implementation.

Downloads come straight from Maven repos (scijava.public proxies Central), SHA1-verified — sites.imagej.net stops re-hosting Maven-born JARs entirely. Uploads for Maven content become “do a Maven release”; the GUI upload flow remains for non-Maven files.

Non-Maven content keeps today’s <plugin filename=...> semantics untouched, in the same index, with filename shadowing as-is. A script or macro that needs a library can declare <dependency coordinate=...>, which just adds a depth-1 root. The genuinely messy spot — and I’d call it the main design risk — is cross-regime collisions: a legacy site ships imglib2-5.0.jar as a file while mediation selects net.imglib2:imglib2:6.2.0. The existing FileObject.getFilename(stripVersion) machinery can detect the collision; I’d rule that coordinate entries win and the file entry surfaces as “overridden” in the UI. This is no worse than today’s silent shadowing, but it deserves explicit design attention because the transition period will exercise it constantly.

How much pre-resolution to bake in — the direct answer

Bake in model building (per-component, per-platform, at publish time, via jgo). Leave graph mediation to the client. Full client-side resolution (fetching and interpreting POMs in Java) buys nothing over this — the flattened facts are lossless for consumption purposes — and costs you the model-building swamp in the bootstrap-critical component, plus a POM-fetch storm at update-check time. Full pre-resolution (db-xml-maven as designed) loses composability, which you’ve correctly identified as the point. The publish step that remains is not the “rigamarole” of resolving whole sites — it’s flattening one component’s model, cheap enough to run as a GitHub Action any third-party developer can point at their G:A:V. (A variant worth considering later: attach the flattened metadata to the Maven release itself as a classifier artifact, so a pure-Maven update site is just “G:A” and metadata distribution rides Maven’s own channel with no site server at all.)

Sequencing and open questions

The standalone-updater / update-the-updater work is still the prerequisite — new client mechanics should reach existing installations through a bootstrap-safe channel before any site starts publishing coordinate entries, and old clients will NPE-adjacent on <dependency> without filename (XMLFileReader.java:172 passes the raw attribute to addDependency), so new-style elements must be invisible to old parsers — new element names, not new attributes on old elements, which the sketch above already respects.

Two things I’d want your read on. First, whether the core Fiji site flips wholesale in one release (clean, but big-bang) or runs dual-emission for a while (the legacy view generated by the jgo flattener — the one place db-xml-maven’s original concept briefly lives on). Second, a strategic fork: if Fiji’s distribution is heading toward shipping a Python runtime anyway (the Jaunch/appose trajectory), delegating even mediation to embedded jgo becomes plausible and the Java mediation port becomes unnecessary. I wouldn’t couple the Updater’s future to that bet — a few hundred lines of well-tested Java is cheap insurance — but if the bet is already placed, it changes the answer.

You 2026-07-03 22:43:55

This makes a lot of sense. But one thing is bothering me: if we flatten the dependency graph for each component, subgraphs won’t get pruned when mediation happens. Here’s an example of what I mean:

honey:1
- bee:1
  - stinger:1
- flower:1
- comb:1
honey:2
- bee:2
- flower:2
- comb:2

Observe that bee dropped the stinger dependency when it moved from version 1 to 2.

Now suppose the user wants to install cereal and toast extensions – and that cereal depends on honey:1, and toast depends on honey:2.

If we use something like Go’s “Minimum Version Selection” (MVS), we’d have the following composed dependency set: cereal, toast, honey:2, bee:2, flower:2, comb:2. There is no stinger:1 because nothing at these versions needs it – that subgraph has been pruned.

But if we flatten each graph, we would lose the context that stinger:1 is only needed because of bee:1; even with MVS over the two flat lists, we’d end up keeping stinger:1 because we forgot the layers of transitivity.

Am I misunderstanding your suggestion here about the dependency graph flattening?

Claude 2026-07-03 22:44:43

You’re misunderstanding it in exactly the way my word choice invited — “flatten” was a bad term. Let me restate precisely: each <release> entry lists only that component’s direct dependencies, not its transitive closure. What gets flattened away is the POM machinery (inheritance, property interpolation, profile activation, dependencyManagement effects — everything needed to answer “what are this component’s direct deps, concretely, per platform”). The graph’s edges are preserved. The index is an adjacency list, and the client walks it.

So in your example, the published facts are per-node:

<component coordinate="brk:honey">
  <release version="1">
    <dependency coordinate="brk:bee:1"/>
    <dependency coordinate="brk:flower:1"/>
    <dependency coordinate="brk:comb:1"/>
  </release>
  <release version="2">
    <dependency coordinate="brk:bee:2"/>
    <dependency coordinate="brk:flower:2"/>
    <dependency coordinate="brk:comb:2"/>
  </release>
</component>
<component coordinate="brk:bee">
  <release version="1">
    <dependency coordinate="brk:stinger:1"/>
  </release>
  <release version="2"/>   <!-- bee:2 dropped stinger -->
</component>

The client’s resolution starts from the roots ({cereal, toast}), mediates honey → 2, and then traverses only honey:2’s edge list, reaching bee:2, whose edge list is empty. stinger:1 is never reached, so it’s never selected — pruning falls out of the graph walk for free, under MVS, nearest-wins, or any other mediation you pick. This is the same reason Maven itself prunes correctly: the loser’s subtree simply isn’t traversed. Losing pruning is what would happen under the db-xml-maven-as-designed scheme (pre-resolved closures per site) — it’s one more reason that design doesn’t compose, and it’s precisely what publishing edges instead of closures fixes.

This does surface a distinction I left implicit and should make explicit in the schema: an entry in the index is a fact, not an installation instruction. stinger:1 does appear in cereal’s site index — as a catalog node with its edges — it just doesn’t get selected. So the index needs to distinguish the components a site offers (the roots a user can enable, what current means today) from the nodes it merely references to make the graph walkable. A site’s publishable obligation is: edge lists for the transitive closure of everything it offers, at every version it mentions. That’s what makes the composed union closed — any version mediation can select arrived via some site’s edge, and that site published the target’s own edges too.

Two fine-print caveats on that closure property:

  • Version ranges break it: a range can resolve to a version nobody published edges for. Ranges are already effectively banned in the SciJava ecosystem (pom-scijava pins everything), so I’d make that a hard rule of the format: edges carry concrete versions only, enforced at publish time.
  • Root overrides: if the user (or the core BOM) pins a version that no enabled site’s graph mentions — say forcing bee:1.5 — its edges are unknown. The clean answer is that pins must resolve within the published catalog, or the pinning site/BOM must publish the missing node’s facts. The core site publishing edge lists for everything its BOM manages covers the realistic cases.

So your instinct about what’s needed was right — MVS-style reasoning requires the layered graph — and the design keeps exactly that. The only thing computed at publish time is what each node’s edges are; which nodes end up in the installation is always computed at composition time, on the user’s machine.

You 2026-07-03 22:50:39

Gotcha. What about Maven dependency exclusions? Do they influence the design of the new <component> schema?

Claude 2026-07-03 22:51:44

Yes — exclusions are the one piece of POM machinery that cannot be resolved away at publish time, so they must be first-class in the schema. Here’s why, and the three places they touch the design.

Why they must travel to the client. An exclusion is path-dependent: cereal → honey ⊘{stinger} means “prune anything matching stinger anywhere in the subtree hanging off this edge.” Which subtree that is depends on which honey version mediation selects — a compose-time outcome. So an exclusion can’t be baked into published facts as “stinger removed”; it has to be recorded on the edge and applied during the client’s walk. The good news is that this fits the “ship the facts, compute the choice” line perfectly: exclusions are per-edge facts, declared verbatim in POMs (post-interpolation — they can contain properties, so model building still has to run first). Publishing captures them losslessly:

<dependency coordinate="brk:bee:1">
  <exclude>org.noisy:stinger</exclude>
  <exclude>com.legacy:*</exclude>      <!-- Maven allows wildcards; keep G:A only, no version/classifier -->
</dependency>

Where they touch the design:

  1. The client walk gets path-sensitive. Plain BFS becomes: each traversal path accumulates the union of exclusion sets along its edges; a node is pruned on a path whose accumulated set matches its G:A; a node stays in the graph iff some surviving path reaches it. And critically, exclusions must apply during graph construction, interleaved with mediation — an excluded node isn’t a version candidate via that path, which can change which version wins elsewhere. This is the second-hardest part of the client mediation code after nearest-wins ordering, and it’s where Maven’s own behavior has historical warts (order-dependence in ties). It’s also exactly the code jgo already has and tests — so this is the strongest argument yet for the golden-test-vectors-from-jgo approach: exclusion×mediation interplay is precisely the kind of thing you don’t want two independently-drifting implementations of.

  2. The <managed> BOM element needs exclusions too. In Maven 3, the root project’s dependencyManagement entries apply to transitive dependencies — including their exclusions. Since the installation is the synthetic root and the core site’s BOM plays the role of its depMgmt, managed entries must be able to carry <exclude> children, applied globally rather than per-path. Note this is only the root’s depMgmt: intermediate nodes’ depMgmt is already accounted for in their own published edge lists (each node’s model building bakes in its own management), so no other depMgmt information needs to travel. (Worth noting Maven 4 is revisiting some transitive-depMgmt semantics — one more reason to declare jgo the normative reference for this format rather than “whatever mvn does this year.”)

  3. A free unification for user intent. The Updater today has ad hoc mechanics for “I don’t want this file even though something depends on it” (the overrides attribute on old-style dependencies, and per-file keep-as-is state). In the new model, that’s just a root-level exclusion — user preferences, BOM curation, and POM-declared exclusions all compose through one mechanism, with well-defined semantics for what happens when they collide (an exclusion prunes transitive reachability but never removes an explicitly-selected root, matching Maven).

One reassurance on the earlier closure property: exclusions don’t threaten it. They only ever remove nodes from the walk — they can’t cause mediation to land on a version whose edges nobody published. So the publishable obligation stays as stated; the pruned graph is always a subgraph of the published union.

The main schema consequence, then, is just the two elements above plus wildcard support (g:*, *:*), and a conformance-test suite generated from jgo covering the nasty interleavings: exclusion changes the mediation winner, same node excluded on one path but reachable via another, wildcard excluding a BOM-managed component, and exclusion of something the user explicitly selected.

Whereas if the db.xml.gz just refers to the G:A:V, clients can do what they want with that, and the db.xml.gz will be far more concise to read. # List of Update Sites

In ImageJ2, anyone can open the Updater and install features from a list of available update sites which is populated by this repository.

## Adding / Editing / Removing an Update Site

If you want to add, edit, or remove an update site from this list, please create a pull request.

1. Fork this repository
2. Modify `sites.yml` in your fork
3. Create a pull request containing your changes

Before adding a new site, please [create a wiki page](https://imagej.net/editing/):

* If your site primarily ships plugins, then for each plugin, use: `https://imagej.net/plugins/your-plugin-name-in-web-case`
* If you want a dedicated page about your update site itself (perhaps linking to all the plugins it offers), use: `https://imagej.net/update-sites/your-update-site-name-in-web-case`

Need help? Don't worry, and post your question on the [Image.sc Forum](https://forum.image.sc/)!

Claude 2026-07-04 02:03:34

Both halves of your message have crisp answers, and they reinforce each other.

The “poor man’s POM” concern — the asymmetry runs the other way

Start from the conservation law you stated yourself: something must do model resolution. The only design freedom is where and when it executes, and that determines what happens when it’s wrong:

  • Publish-time bug: the buggy output is a gzipped text file, derived deterministically from immutable POMs. Fix the flattener, rerun the workflow, every site’s <component> blocks are corrected, and every client picks up the fix on its next update check — no client code changes, no user action.
  • Client-time bug: the buggy resolution lives in shipped Java code, distributed through the slowest and most dangerous channel in the entire system — self-updating clients, with a user population that runs years-old versions. And until every client updates, different installations resolve the same site differently, which is a reproducibility failure worse than any frozen flattener bug.

So “baked into db.xml.gz” is the recoverable failure mode. The db.xml.gz isn’t a second source of truth — it’s a materialized view with provenance: each entry carries its G:A:V, so anyone (including a future, smarter client!) can re-derive and verify the edges from the POM. Embedding the facts forecloses nothing; a client that distrusts them can ignore them. Three cheap mitigations make this airtight: stamp each index with generator-version and a format version so buggy outputs are identifiable and auditable; have the publish pipeline cross-validate jgo’s flattening against mvn dependency:list before publishing (a pombast-flavored gate); and keep golden tests pinning flattener behavior.

One genuine concession your concern forces: it kills the variant I floated earlier of attaching the flattened graph to the Maven release as a classifier artifact. Release repositories are immutable — a buggy fact file deployed there could never be fixed. Facts must live in regenerable locations, i.e., the site indexes. (If index size ever bothers you, the facts can split into a companion graph.xml.gz next to a lean db.xml.gz — but that’s a transport detail with cache-coherency cost; I wouldn’t start there. Gzipped adjacency lists are small.)

The workflow — yes, your sketch is the design

Extending sites.yml is exactly right, and it’s satisfying how little new infrastructure it needs:

- name: "TrackMate"
  id: "TrackMate"
  url: "https://sites.imagej.net/TrackMate/"
  source: "sc.fiji:TrackMate"        # optionally :7.12.0 to pin, default = latest release
  maintainers: [...]

The recast db-xml-maven tool — the true rump of it, a jgo client that resolves the seed’s closure per platform and emits <component>/<managed> blocks — gets invoked by automation with three triggers:

  1. sites.yml changes — the existing deploy workflow, extended: a PR adding source: is how a developer “marks a G:A as the seed of an update site.” That PR is the entire onboarding ceremony, which is a lovely property.
  2. New release deployments — a scheduled sweep comparing maven-metadata.xml lastUpdated against each site’s index stamp (cheap; this is the same polling pattern the status.scijava.org infrastructure already does), regenerating only stale sites.
  3. On-demand — repository_dispatch, so a component’s own release CI can poke list-of-update-sites for immediate regeneration instead of waiting for the sweep, plus manual workflow_dispatch.

Two operational details matter. First, regeneration is a merge, not an overwrite: the generator owns the <component> and <managed> blocks; hand-uploaded non-Maven entries (scripts, LUTs, sample data — which the Fiji core site will always have) stay owned by the existing GUI upload flow, and the generator must round-trip them untouched. Second, the tool must also work outside this repo: list-of-update-sites’ workflow can only write to sites it hosts (sites.imagej.net); self-hosted sites need the same tool as a CLI or reusable GitHub Action they run themselves, with sites.yml merely registering the result. Same code, two invocation contexts.

So, answering your question directly: yes — something must always do the POM resolution and write the component blocks somewhere, and yes, it must re-run on new deployments. The design choice you’re making is only that this happens once per release, centrally, in regenerable form rather than millions of times in versioned-skewed client code. The source: key in sites.yml is where the two paradigms finally meet: an update site becomes a published, continuously-maintained view of a Maven project, and the Updater client composes those views with Maven semantics without ever touching a POM.

You 2026-07-04 02:30:16

Note that list-of-update-sites can list urls other than only sites.imagej.net – anyone can file a PR to add a site hosted anywhere. That said, the list-of-update-sites sites.yml index is still analogous to an apt source or Homebrew tap or conda channel (and each “update site” analogous to a package). We could at some point enhance the Updater to support multiple such sources/taps/channels, rather than only the one hardcoded one. But that is a project for another time, yeah?

It is tempting to try to teach the Updater to support something like a maven://groupId/artifactId (or maven://repositoryUrl/groupId/artifactId) scheme directly when adding an update site, but it would be problematic for a couple of reasons: 1) none of the model resolution work we’ve been talking about would be done yet; and 2) there would be no great place to attach additional metadata about the update site beyond only its Maven repository coordinate, whereas with sites.yml we can freely add more keys later for the Updater to harvest and use when presenting extensions to the user.

Remaining questions: 1. How should this revised maven-updater tooling handle the “back catalog” of a Maven project’s previous releases? 2. Fiji has long been unable to do plugin/component/extension downgrades, which limits its reproducibility. It feels potentially feasible to tackle that as part of this work. My original db-xml-maven design would have supported it by having each V of a G:A be its own update site; how could we do it with this revised approach? Or do you think it’s out of scope?

Claude 2026-07-04 02:31:34

Agreed on both preliminaries. The sources/taps multiplexing is cleanly separable — nothing in the source: design forecloses it, since a second registry is just another sites.yml the client subscribes to — so yes, another time. And the maven:// scheme is an attractive nuisance for exactly your two reasons, plus a third: it’s client-side resolution sneaking back in through the back door. A bare coordinate leaves no place for the curation — the BOM, the non-Maven files, the back-catalog policy, the regenerable facts — which is precisely what the registry entry + generated index provide.

1. The back catalog

Here’s the pleasant surprise: the revision makes the back catalog cheap, because the two things that made it expensive in db-xml-maven’s design are both gone. The crazy checksum forced downloading every JAR ever released (the README’s “multiple GB” worry #2); SHA1s are precomputed repo metadata — tiny .sha1 files sitting next to every artifact. And pre-resolved closures forced recomputing entire site states per version; per-node edge lists only require POMs. So cataloging a project’s full history means fetching POMs and .sha1 files — kilobytes per version — and on maven.scijava.org with Nexus storage access, not even HTTP.

Concretely, I’d handle it with two grades of entry and one policy knob:

  • Full entries (edges, sha1, min-java, description) for every release version of the seed G:A and everything reachable in those versions’ graphs. This is what mediation and downgrades consume. The version list comes from maven-metadata.xml; generation is incremental and append-only — facts derived from immutable POMs never change, so the sweep only computes the new release’s subgraph and appends.
  • Slim entries (version + sha1 + filename only, no edges, marked non-installable) as a fallback for ancient versions whose POMs won’t resolve cleanly — pre-Mavenized junk, malformed 2010-era parents. jgo’s lenient mode plus slim-entry fallback guarantees generation never hard-fails on archaeology, while still letting the client recognize an old installed file as “TrackMate 2.1.0, released 2013” instead of “unknown locally modified file.” That recognition role is the legitimate successor of <previous-version>.
  • A per-site floor in sites.yml (catalog-since: "2.0.0" or a date) for projects whose deep history is genuinely worthless, defaulting to “everything.”

The one truly non-Maven part of the back catalog — hand-built binaries people uploaded directly over the years — stays as legacy <plugin> entries with the old checksums, harvested once from the existing core db.xml.gz files (the surviving kernel of db-xml-maven task #7). That’s a one-time import, really only needed for the core sites.

2. Downgrades — in scope, and nearly free

The per-V-update-site design was simulating, with server-side machinery, what client-side mediation gives you natively: in the composed-root model, a downgrade is just a pinned root edge. Today “TrackMate enabled” means the synthetic root depends on the site’s current version; a downgrade means it depends on sc.fiji:TrackMate:7.10.0 explicitly. Mediation re-runs over the composed graph and the whole subtree re-resolves consistently — older imglib2 if the edges say so, pruning and exclusions included. No extra sites, no extra server machinery. The marginal cost is exactly three things: a version dropdown in the UI (populated from the back catalog — questions 1 and 2 are the same feature, the catalog is the candidate list, filtered by min-java), a defined precedence (user pin > core BOM > mediation, with a visible warning when a pin drags a managed library below what other extensions expect), and pin persistence.

That last item is where I’d encourage you to aim slightly higher, because it’s the actual reproducibility prize. The local state file becomes an installation manifest: the list of (site, coordinate, optional pin) roots. That’s small, human-readable, and composable — share it and someone else’s Updater materializes the same intent. But intent isn’t full reproducibility, since unpinned components drift as sites update. So mirror jgo’s own two-artifact pattern (jgo.toml + lockfile): the manifest for composable intent, plus an exportable lockfile recording the entire mediated selection with versions and SHA1s for byte-exact reconstruction. “Export this installation” / “reconstruct from lockfile” falls out of machinery you’re building anyway, and it finally gives methods sections and container builds something better than “Fiji, downloaded sometime in March.”

So: in scope — with a sequencing caveat. Design it in from day one, because it’s load-bearing for the schema (per-version entries, pins in the manifest format) and retrofitting per-version facts later would mean regenerating every index. But ship it as a fast-follow: land install/update/compose on the new mediation first, prove that stable, then expose the version dropdown and lockfile export. The dangerous part of downgrades was never the mechanism — it was doing them atop a model that couldn’t reason about versions. That’s the part this whole design fixes.

# Maven-Native Update Sites: Design and Implementation Plan

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | DRAFT — for discussion |
| **Date** | 2026-07-03 |
| **Scope** | imagej-updater, list-of-update-sites, new publisher tooling (jgo-based), fiji/fiji release process |

## 1. Summary

Teach the ImageJ Updater to understand Maven coordinates as first-class
citizens, so that update sites compose with Maven semantics rather than
filename shadowing. The design splits Maven dependency resolution at a
principled line:

- **Model building** (POM inheritance, property interpolation, profile
  activation, dependencyManagement effects) runs **once, at publish time**,
  via [jgo](https://github.com/apposed/jgo)-based tooling, producing
  per-component *direct dependency* facts embedded in each site's index.
- **Graph mediation** (version selection over the composed installation)
  runs **on the client**, so that enabling multiple update sites behaves
  exactly like a Maven project depending on all selected extensions.

Slogan: **ship the facts, compute the choice.**

This supersedes the [db-xml-maven](https://github.com/ctrueden/db-xml-maven)
prototype. Its `maven.py` half already graduated into jgo v2; its
generation design (one pre-resolved update site per project version) is
replaced by this document, because pre-resolved closures do not compose:
cross-site conflicts degrade to filename shadowing, and pruned subgraphs
(dependencies dropped between versions) cannot be recovered from flattened
lists.

## 2. Design principles

1. **The installation is a synthetic root project.** Every component the
   user has selected, across all enabled sites, is a direct (depth-1)
   dependency of that root. The core site's BOM plays the role of the
   root's `<dependencyManagement>`. This is the same operation jgo's
   `PythonResolver.resolve(list)` performs and the same composition
   `pombast melt` validates.
2. **Publish edges, not closures.** Each published `<release>` lists only
   that component's direct dependencies (post model-building). The client
   reconstructs the composed graph by walking edges, so mediation prunes
   subgraphs correctly (a dependency dropped in v2 is never reached when
   v2 is selected).
3. **The index is a materialized view, not a source of truth.** Every
   entry carries its G:A:V; anyone can re-derive and verify the facts from
   the POM. Facts are regenerable: a flattener bug is fixed by rerunning
   the generator, not by shipping client code. Consequently, facts must
   live only in regenerable locations (site indexes) — never in immutable
   release repositories.
4. **Coexistence, not replacement.** Filename-keyed `<plugin>` entries
   keep today's semantics untouched, in the same index. Non-Maven content
   (scripts, macros, LUTs, launchers, hand-uploaded historical JARs) and
   legacy third-party sites continue to work unchanged.
5. **jgo is the normative reference.** Client mediation is validated
   against golden test vectors generated by jgo, not against "whatever
   mvn does this year."

## 3. Index format (draft)

New elements live alongside existing `db.xml.gz` content. Old clients
ignore unknown *elements*; new-style data must never appear as new
attributes on old elements (`XMLFileReader` would pass a null `filename`
to `FileObject.addDependency`).

```xml
<component coordinate="sc.fiji:TrackMate" offered="true" current="7.12.0">
  <release version="7.12.0" sha1="..." filesize="..." timestamp="..." min-java="8">
    <description>...</description>
    <dependency coordinate="net.imglib2:imglib2:6.2.0"/>
    <dependency coordinate="org.jogamp.gluegen:gluegen-rt:2.4.0"
                classifier="natives-linux-amd64" platform="linux64"/>
    <dependency coordinate="com.google.guava:guava:32.1.2-jre">
      <exclude>com.google.code.findbugs:jsr305</exclude>
    </dependency>
  </release>
  <release version="7.11.0" sha1="..." min-java="8">...</release>
  <!-- slim entry: recognized but not installable (POM unresolvable) -->
  <release version="2.1.0" sha1="..." slim="true"/>
</component>

<managed bom="org.scijava:pom-scijava:45.0.0">
  <version coordinate="net.imglib2:imglib2:6.2.0"/>
  <version coordinate="com.google.guava:guava:32.1.2-jre">
    <exclude>com.google.code.findbugs:jsr305</exclude>
  </version>
</managed>
```

Rules and semantics:

- **Identity** is G:A(:C:P); version is a first-class variable, not a
  filename substring. Filenames are derived (`jars/artifactId-version.jar`
  conventions preserved).
- **Offered vs referenced.** `offered="true"` marks components a user can
  enable (site roots). All other `<component>` entries are catalog facts
  needed to make the graph walkable. Presence in the index is *not* an
  installation instruction; the installed set is always the output of
  mediation.
- **Closure obligation.** A site publishes edge lists for the transitive
  closure of everything it offers, at every full-entry version it
  mentions. This makes the composed union closed: any version mediation
  can select arrived via some site's edge, and that site published the
  target's own edges.
- **Concrete versions only.** Version ranges are forbidden in published
  edges (enforced at publish time). Ranges would break the closure
  property.
- **Exclusions** are per-edge facts (post-interpolation), G:A only,
  wildcards allowed (`g:*`, `*:*`). They cannot be resolved away at
  publish time because their effect is path-dependent and depends on
  compose-time mediation. Root-level (`<managed>`) exclusions apply
  globally, matching Maven 3 root-depMgmt semantics. Intermediate nodes'
  depMgmt is already baked into their own published edges.
- **Platform-specific dependencies** are pre-expanded at publish time by
  resolving under each supported platform's `ProfileConstraints` and
  diffing, emitted with `platform` attributes using the Updater's short
  platform names.
- **Scopes** are pre-filtered at publish time (compile/runtime only).
- **Checksums**: SHA1 (from repo `.sha1` metadata) for both download
  verification and local-modification detection of Maven-born files. The
  legacy content-aware JAR digest remains only for filename-keyed legacy
  entries. Rationale: Maven release artifacts are immutable; "rebuilt
  locally but equivalent" should show as modified.
- **Provenance stamps**: each index records `generator` name+version and a
  format version, so buggy generator outputs are identifiable and bulk
  regeneration is auditable.

## 4. Client: composition and mediation

### 4.1 Mediation algorithm

**OPEN DECISION — must be settled before format freeze.** Candidates:

- **(a) Maven semantics** (nearest-wins, first-declared tie-break, root
  depMgmt override): faithful to how the components were built and
  tested together (pom-scijava, pombast melt), and jgo is a ready-made
  reference implementation for golden tests. Quirk: order-dependence in
  ties.
- **(b) Minimum Version Selection** (Go-style): more predictable, but
  diverges from the ecosystem's build-time behavior and has no defined
  interplay with exclusions; jgo could not serve as the oracle.
- **(c) Highest-wins** (Gradle-style): simple, but same divergence
  concern as (b).

**Recommendation: (a).** The core design promise is "the composed
installation behaves as if you built a Maven project depending on the
selected extensions." Divergent mediation quietly breaks that promise and
forfeits the reference implementation.

Precedence in all cases: **user pin > core BOM (`<managed>`) > mediation**.
An exclusion prunes transitive reachability but never removes an
explicitly selected root. Pins that drag a managed library below what
other enabled extensions expect produce a visible warning, not silent
success.

### 4.2 Algorithm sketch

1. Fetch enabled sites' indexes (one HTTP request per site, as today).
2. Roots = user-selected offered components (with any pins) + root
   exclusions (user "never install X" preferences).
3. Walk the union graph from the roots. Each path accumulates the union
   of exclusion sets along its edges; matching nodes are pruned on that
   path. Exclusion filtering interleaves with mediation (an excluded node
   is not a version candidate via that path).
4. Mediate versions per G:A:C:P; traverse only the winner's edge list.
5. Filter release candidates by `min-java` against the running JVM.
6. Diff the mediated selection against local state (filename+SHA1
   recognition; slim entries let ancient installs be identified rather
   than reported as unknown files).
7. Download Maven-born files directly from Maven repositories
   (scijava.public proxies Central), SHA1-verified. sites.imagej.net
   hosts only non-Maven files and indexes.

Implementation: Java port of the mediation walk (a few hundred lines +
Maven's `ComparableVersion` ordering), validated by golden vectors (§7).
Full model building stays out of the client permanently.

*Contingency:* if Fiji's distribution commits to shipping a Python
runtime (Jaunch/appose trajectory), delegating mediation to embedded jgo
becomes an option; the Java port is cheap insurance and should proceed
regardless.

### 4.3 Mixed-regime collisions

A legacy filename entry (e.g. `jars/imglib2-5.0.jar` from an old-style
site) can collide with a mediated coordinate selection. Detection uses
the existing version-stripping logic (`FileObject.getFilename(true)`).
Rule: **coordinate entries win**; the file entry surfaces as
"overridden" in the UI rather than silently shadowing. This is the
riskiest area of the design and gets dedicated tests (§7).

### 4.4 Manifest and lockfile

Local state becomes two artifacts, mirroring jgo's `jgo.toml` + lockfile:

- **Installation manifest**: the roots — (site, coordinate, optional
  pin) — plus root exclusions. Small, human-readable, composable;
  sharing it reproduces *intent*.
- **Exportable lockfile**: the full mediated selection with versions and
  SHA1s, for byte-exact reconstruction of an installation (methods
  sections, containers).

### 4.5 Downgrades

A downgrade is a pinned root edge: the synthetic root depends on
`G:A:7.10.0` instead of the site's `current`. Mediation re-runs and the
subtree re-resolves consistently (pruning included). Marginal cost: a
version dropdown (populated from the back catalog, filtered by
`min-java`), pin persistence in the manifest, and the precedence warning
above. Designed in from day one (schema + manifest must support it);
shipped as a fast-follow after core install/update/compose is stable.

## 5. Publisher tooling

A jgo-based **flattener** (the surviving kernel of db-xml-maven,
heavily recast): given a seed G:A (or G:A:V), resolve the closure per
platform, emit `<component>`/`<managed>` blocks, and **merge** them into
the site index — generator owns coordinate blocks; GUI-uploaded
filename entries are round-tripped untouched (the core Fiji site will
always have both).

Packaged two ways from the same code:

1. **CLI + reusable GitHub Action** — any developer can generate/refresh
   their own self-hosted site.
2. **list-of-update-sites integration** — `sites.yml` gains a site-level
   `source:` key:

   ```yaml
   - name: "TrackMate"
     id: "TrackMate"
     url: "https://sites.imagej.net/TrackMate/"
     source: "sc.fiji:TrackMate"      # optionally :7.12.0 to pin
     catalog-since: "3.0.0"           # optional back-catalog floor
     maintainers: [...]
   ```

   Registering a Maven-seeded site = one PR adding `source:`. The
   workflow regenerates indexes it hosts (sites.imagej.net) on three
   triggers: sites.yml changes; a scheduled sweep comparing
   `maven-metadata.xml` `lastUpdated` against index stamps; and
   `repository_dispatch` so component release CI can request immediate
   regeneration.

**Back catalog**: full entries (edges + sha1) for every release version
of the seed and everything reachable from those versions — cheap,
because only POMs and `.sha1` files are fetched, never JARs. Generation
is incremental/append-only (facts from immutable POMs never change).
Unresolvable ancient versions degrade to slim entries via jgo lenient
mode; `catalog-since` floors genuinely worthless history. Historical
hand-uploaded binaries are imported once from existing core `db.xml.gz`
files as legacy entries.

**Validation gate**: before publishing, cross-check the flattener's
per-component direct deps against `mvn` output (pombast-style), and
verify provenance stamps.

**Uploads**: Maven-born content is published by doing a Maven release.
The GUI upload flow remains for non-Maven files.

## 6. Migration and sequencing

- **Phase 0 — de-risking spike + bootstrap safety.**
  - Spike the flattener: generate real indexes for `sc.fiji:TrackMate`
    and `sc.fiji:fiji`; eyeball size, closure correctness, platform
    expansion, back-catalog behavior on ugly old POMs.
  - Generate first golden mediation vectors from jgo.
  - Standalone updater / "update the updater" channel (long-planned):
    new client mechanics must reach existing installations through a
    bootstrap-safe path before any site publishes new-style entries.
- **Phase 1 — format freeze.** Settle §4.1; freeze schema + manifest
  format (including pins); publish the conformance test suite.
- **Phase 2 — client + core site.** Java mediation engine behind the new
  updater; core Fiji site emits coordinate blocks (dual emission with
  legacy view during transition — **open question below**). Downloads
  move to Maven repos; sites.imagej.net can bridge old clients with
  `filename-timestamp` → Nexus redirects.
- **Phase 3 — ecosystem.** `source:` key + workflow triggers live in
  list-of-update-sites; reusable Action for self-hosted sites;
  third-party onboarding docs.
- **Phase 4 — reproducibility features.** Version dropdown (downgrades),
  lockfile export/import, min-java-aware upgrade guidance.

## 7. Testing strategy

- **Golden vectors from jgo** (normative reference): composed inputs →
  expected selection, generated in CI (e.g. via `uv run`), consumed by
  imagej-updater's Java tests. Must cover the nasty interleavings:
  exclusion flips the mediation winner; node excluded on one path,
  reachable via another; wildcard exclusion of a BOM-managed component;
  pin below other extensions' needs; pruned-subgraph case (the
  bee/stinger example: a dependency dropped between versions must not
  survive mediation to the newer version).
- **Flattener validation**: per-component direct deps diffed against
  `mvn dependency:list` across a corpus including pathological POMs
  (interpolated G/A fields, platform profiles, BOM imports).
- **Mixed-regime tests**: legacy site + coordinate site colliding on the
  same library; overridden-file UI state.
- **End-to-end**: fresh install, 3-year-old install recognition/upgrade,
  compose two third-party sites with conflicting transitive deps,
  downgrade + lockfile round-trip.

## 8. Open questions

1. **Mediation algorithm** (§4.1) — recommendation: Maven semantics.
2. **Core-site cutover**: flip wholesale in one Fiji release (clean,
   big-bang) vs dual emission for a transition period (legacy view
   produced by the flattener — the one place db-xml-maven's original
   concept briefly survives).
3. **Embedded-Python contingency** (§4.2): is Fiji committing to shipping
   a Python runtime? Does not block the Java mediation port.
4. **Transport split**: single `db.xml.gz` vs lean `db.xml.gz` + companion
   `graph.xml.gz`. Default: single file; revisit only if size becomes
   real.
5. **Multiple registries** (apt-source/tap/channel analogy for
   list-of-update-sites): explicitly out of scope; nothing here
   forecloses it.

## 9. Work breakdown by repository

| Repo | Work |
|---|---|
| **imagej-updater** | Schema reader/writer for new elements; mediation engine + `ComparableVersion`; manifest/lockfile; SHA1 local-state recognition; Maven-repo downloader; mixed-regime collision handling; UI (overridden state, version dropdown, warnings); golden-vector test harness. |
| **new: updater-site-generator** (name TBD) | jgo-based flattener: closure walk, per-platform expansion, slim-entry fallback, merge-not-overwrite emission, provenance stamps, validation gate; CLI + reusable GitHub Action. |
| **list-of-update-sites** | `source:`/`catalog-since:` schema; regeneration workflow (push, schedule, dispatch); hosted-site publishing. |
| **fiji/fiji** | Core site BOM publication (from the POM, pombast-adjacent); release CI dispatch hook; retire `populate-app.sh` + manual upload. |
| **jgo** | Golden-vector emitter (small; possibly lives in the generator repo instead). |
| **ctrueden/db-xml-maven** | Retire; point README here. |

Claude 2026-07-04 02:40:04

Yes — and I’ve written it: doc/maven-native-update-sites.md in this repo (new doc/ directory, uncommitted so you can review and relocate if you’d rather it live elsewhere, e.g. as a forum RFC or in a new generator repo).

It captures everything we converged on — the ship-the-facts/compute-the-choice split, the <component>/<managed> schema draft with exclusions and slim entries, the closure rules, client mediation with precedence (pin > BOM > mediation), manifest + lockfile, back-catalog policy, the source: key and regeneration triggers for list-of-update-sites, mixed-regime collision handling, a four-phase migration plan, testing strategy, and a per-repo work breakdown.

Three things in it genuinely need your decision rather than more design work, and I’d settle them before anything else:

  1. Mediation algorithm — Maven nearest-wins vs MVS vs highest-wins. I’ve recommended Maven semantics, since “the installation behaves like a Maven project” is the whole promise and it makes jgo usable as the test oracle, but you floated MVS approvingly and this is the one choice that’s expensive to change after format freeze.
  2. Core-site cutover — big-bang flip in one Fiji release vs dual emission during a transition window.
  3. The embedded-Python contingency — whether Fiji’s distribution trajectory changes the client implementation story (it doesn’t block the Java mediation port either way).

For actual next work, the plan’s Phase 0 is deliberately a de-risking spike, and I’d start there before polishing the document further: run the flattener concept for real against sc.fiji:TrackMate and sc.fiji:fiji using jgo’s API (index size, closure walk, per-platform expansion, and how ugly the 2012-era back-catalog POMs actually get in lenient mode), and generate the first golden mediation vectors from jgo — including the bee/stinger pruning case. Those two experiments will tell you quickly whether any part of the design needs revisiting, and both are afternoon-sized. I’m happy to build either spike whenever you want.

  1. I think “MVS” and “highest-wins” are the same thing here? And I think it’s going to be more robust for Fiji than nearest-wins. For each component we should match exactly Maven’s behavior as jgo strives to do: the <component> blocks we generate will match Maven behavior. But on the Updater client side, when composing update sites, let’s use MVS/highest-wins – and if we have a SemVer-major-version misalignment, raise that so the user knows this combination may be incompatible!

  2. We’ll set up a test update site to replace the current one, to avoid disrupting production. Once the replacement seamlessly slots in, we can swap over. As for “dual emission”: do you mean a hybrid site driven by sc.fiji:fiji + legacy non-Maven entries? If so, I think we need to keep the legacy non-Maven entries indefinitely, to avoid everyone having a lot of “locally modified” files when trying to update.

  3. Good question – but I think Fiji is not quite ready to start bundling Python. We should make the Updater mediation happen in Java code for now (as nice as it would be to be able to reuse jgo for this).

Claude 2026-07-04 16:51:26

All three decisions are sound, and one of them makes the client simpler — let me confirm your equivalence question first, then fold everything into the doc.

On (1): yes, in our setting MVS and highest-wins coincide, and it’s worth understanding why, because it’s load-bearing. MVS picks the maximum of the minimum requirements; Gradle-style highest-wins picks the highest requested version. These differ only when requirements can be ranges or upper bounds — and we already banned ranges from published edges (closure property), so every edge is a concrete “at least this” and the two algorithms compute the same fixpoint. The equivalence is a consequence of a rule we already committed to, which is a nice sign the design hangs together. Let me update the document.

Default: single file; revisit only if size becomes\n   real.\n5. **Multiple registries** (apt-source/tap/channel analogy for\n   list-of-update-sites): explicitly out of scope; nothing here\n   forecloses it.\n6. **SemVer warning heuristics**: exact trigger conditions (major-only?\n   0.x handling?) and UI presentation \u2014 decide during Phase 2 UI work."
}

Claude 2026-07-04 16:53:01

The doc is updated — all three decisions are recorded in a decision log (§8), with the design text revised to match. A few things worth noting about the consequences:

On MVS ≡ highest-wins: confirmed, with the precise reason now in §4.1 — they coincide because published edges carry concrete versions only (each a minimum requirement), which the closure rules already mandate. If ranges were ever admitted the two would diverge, so that’s one more reason the range ban stays. Your choice also bought two things for free: the client mediation code shrinks (MVS is a monotone fixpoint — no depth tracking, no declaration-order tie-breaks, and results are provably independent of site enable-order), and it’s simple enough that an independent ~100-line reference implementation can generate the golden test vectors. That was necessary anyway: since jgo implements Maven’s nearest-wins, it stays the oracle for flattener validation but can’t be the oracle for mediation anymore. The exclusion interplay — which Go’s MVS has no concept of — is now explicitly defined in §4.1 as part of the fixpoint. I also generalized your warning idea slightly: it fires both on SemVer-major misalignment and whenever a pin or BOM entry holds a library below what some surviving edge requests.

On “dual emission”: what I meant was narrower than what you described, and your point subsumes it. The test-site index has three content layers, now spelled out in Phase 2: (1) the <component>/<managed> blocks for new clients; (2) legacy <plugin> blocks for the current Maven JARs so pre-new-updater clients keep working — that’s the part I called “dual emission,” and it’s the only temporary layer, retired when old clients are extinct; (3) legacy entries for non-Maven content and historical version recognition — which, as you say, stay indefinitely, precisely to avoid the “wall of locally modified files” failure for long-unattended installations. Indexes are gzipped text; carrying history forever costs nothing.

On Java mediation: recorded as decided, with the embedded-jgo option noted as revisitable but non-blocking.

With the decisions settled, the plan’s critical path starts at Phase 0, and both spikes are now well-defined: the jgo-based flattener run against sc.fiji:TrackMate and sc.fiji:fiji (index size, closure walk, platform expansion, lenient-mode behavior on ancient POMs), and the MVS reference implementation with the first golden vectors — including bee/stinger pruning and enable-order permutation cases. Say the word and I’ll start building either one.
